\displaystyle\partial_{r}\partial_{u}\overset{(1)}{q}_{AB}=\overset{(1)}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)\,r^{(n-9)/2}\partial_{u}h_{AB}+\alpha^{2}\overset{(1)}{\psi}_{[\alpha]}r^{\frac{n-5}{2}}\partial_{u}h_{AB}+m\overset{(1)}{\psi}_{[m]}r^{-(n+5)/2}\partial_{u}h_{AB}\,.

(A.38)

We use (
A.5
) to rewrite this as,

\displaystyle\partial_{r}\bigg[\partial_{u}\overset{(1)}{q}_{AB}-\overset{(1)}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)\,\hat{q}_{AB}^{(n-9)/2}-\alpha^{2}\overset{(1)}{\psi}_{[\alpha]}\hat{q}_{AB}^{\frac{n-5}{2}}-m\overset{(1)}{\psi}_{[m]}\hat{q}_{AB}^{-(n+5)/2}\bigg]

\displaystyle=\overset{(1)}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)\,\bigg[\mathcal{K}\left(\tfrac{n-9}{2},\mathring{\Delta},P\right)r^{(n-11)/2}+\alpha^{2}\mathcal{K}_{[\alpha]}(\tfrac{n-9}{2})r^{(n-7)/2}+m\mathcal{K}_{[m]}(\tfrac{n-9}{2})r^{-(n+7)/2}\bigg]h_{AB}

\displaystyle\quad+\alpha^{2}\overset{(1)}{\psi}_{[\alpha]}\big[\mathcal{K}\left(\tfrac{n-5}{2},\mathring{\Delta},P\right)r^{(n-7)/2}+\alpha^{2}\mathcal{K}_{[\alpha]}(\tfrac{n-5}{2})r^{\frac{n-3}{2}}+m\mathcal{K}_{[m]}(\tfrac{n-5}{2})r^{-(n+3)/2}\big]h_{AB}

\displaystyle\quad+m\overset{(1)}{\psi}_{[m]}\big[\mathcal{K}\left(-\tfrac{n+5}{2},\mathring{\Delta},P\right)r^{-(n+7)/2}+\alpha^{2}\mathcal{K}_{[\alpha]}(-\tfrac{n+5}{2})r^{-(n+3)/2}+m\mathcal{K}_{[m]}(-\tfrac{n+5}{2})r^{-3(n+1)/2}\big]h_{AB}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\overset{(1)}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)\,\mathcal{K}\left(\tfrac{n-9}{2},\mathring{\Delta},P\right)}_{\overset{(2)}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)}r^{(n-11)/2}h_{AB}+\alpha^{4}\underbrace{\overset{(1)}{\psi}_{[\alpha]}\mathcal{K}_{[\alpha]}(\tfrac{n-5}{2})}_{\overset{(2)}{\psi}_{[\alpha]}}r^{\frac{n-3}{2}}h_{AB}

\displaystyle\quad+m^{2}\underbrace{\overset{(1)}{\psi}_{[m]}\mathcal{K}_{[m]}(-\tfrac{n+5}{2})}_{\overset{(2)}{\psi}_{[m]}}r^{-3(n+1)/2}h_{AB}+m\underbrace{\bigg[\overset{(1)}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)\,\mathcal{K}_{[m]}(\tfrac{n-9}{2})+\overset{(1)}{\psi}_{[m]}\mathcal{K}\left(-\tfrac{n+5}{2},\mathring{\Delta},P\right)\big]}_{\overset{(2)}{\psi}_{1,0}(\mathring{\Delta},P)}r^{-(n+7)/2}h_{AB}

\displaystyle\quad+m\alpha^{2}\underbrace{\bigg[\overset{(1)}{\psi}_{[\alpha]}\mathcal{K}_{[m]}(\tfrac{n-5}{2})+\overset{(1)}{\psi}_{[m]}\mathcal{K}_{[\alpha]}(-\tfrac{n+5}{2})\big]}_{\overset{(2)}{\psi}_{1,1}(\mathring{\Delta},P)}r^{-(n+3)/2}h_{AB}\,.

(A.39)

where we made use of the fact that

\displaystyle\overset{(1)}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)\,\mathcal{K}_{[\alpha]}(\tfrac{n-9}{2})+\overset{(1)}{\psi}_{[\alpha]}\mathcal{K}\left(\tfrac{n-5}{2},\mathring{\Delta},P\right)=0\,,

(A.40)

as verified readily using (
A.6
)-(
A.7
), which implies that the two terms involving
r^{(n-7)/2}
in (the first equality of) (
A.39
) cancel, after which we are left with the
i=2
statement.

Continuing in the same way, we take
\partial_{u}
of (
A.28
) and make use again of (
A.5
) to obtain:

\displaystyle\partial_{r}\bigg[\partial_{u}\overset{(i)}{q}_{AB}-\overset{(i)}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)\,\hat{q}_{AB}^{(n-7-2i)/2}-\alpha^{2i}\overset{(i)}{\psi}_{[\alpha]}\hat{q}_{AB}^{(n-7+2i)/2}-m^{i}\overset{(i)}{\psi}_{[m]}\hat{q}^{(\frac{n-7-2i(n-1)}{2})}_{AB}

\displaystyle\qquad-\sum_{j,\ell}^{i_{**}}m^{j}\alpha^{2\ell}\overset{(i)}{\psi}_{j,\ell}(\mathring{\Delta},P)\,\hat{q}^{(\frac{1}{2}(n-7-2i-2j(n-2)+4\ell))}_{AB}\bigg]

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\overset{(i)}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)\,\mathcal{K}\left(\tfrac{n-7-2i}{2},\mathring{\Delta},P\right)}_{\overset{(i+1)}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)}r^{(n-9-2i)/2}h_{AB}+\alpha^{2(i+1)}\underbrace{\overset{(i)}{\psi}_{[\alpha]}\mathcal{K}_{[\alpha]}(\tfrac{n-7+2i}{2})}_{\overset{(i+1)}{\psi}_{[\alpha]}}r^{(n-5+2i)/2}h_{AB}

\displaystyle\quad+m^{i+1}\underbrace{\overset{(i)}{\psi}_{[m]}\mathcal{K}_{[m]}(\tfrac{n-7-2i(n-1)}{2})}_{\overset{(i+1)}{\psi}_{[m]}}r^{\frac{n-7-2(i+1)(n-1)}{2}}h_{AB}

\displaystyle\quad+m\overset{(i)}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)\,\mathcal{K}_{[m]}(\tfrac{n-7-2i}{2})r^{\frac{n-7-2i}{2}-(n-1)}h_{AB}+m^{i}\overset{(i)}{\psi}_{[m]}\mathcal{K}\left(\tfrac{n-7-2i(n-1)}{2},\mathring{\Delta},P\right)r^{\frac{n-7-2i(n-1)}{2}-1}h_{AB}

\displaystyle\quad+m\alpha^{2i}\overset{(i)}{\psi}_{[\alpha]}\mathcal{K}_{[m]}(\tfrac{n-7+2i}{2})r^{\frac{n-7+2i}{2}-(n-1)}h_{AB}+m^{i}\alpha^{2}\overset{(i)}{\psi}_{[m]}\mathcal{K}_{[\alpha]}(\tfrac{n-7-2i(n-1)}{2})r^{\frac{n-7-2i(n-1)}{2}+1}h_{AB}

\displaystyle\quad+\overset{(i)}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)\,\alpha^{2}\mathcal{K}_{[\alpha]}(\tfrac{n-7-2i}{2})r^{(n-5-2i)/2}h_{AB}+\alpha^{2i}\overset{(i)}{\psi}_{[\alpha]}\mathcal{K}\left(\tfrac{n-7+2i}{2},\mathring{\Delta},P\right)r^{(n-9+2i)/2}h_{AB}

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{j,\ell}^{i_{**}}m^{j}\alpha^{2\ell}\overset{(i)}{\psi}_{j,\ell}(\mathring{\Delta},P)[\ldots]\,,

(A.41)

where the terms
[\ldots]
, which have the right structure, have been omitted as their detailed form is irrelevant for further purposes.

A similar argument to that below (
A.26
), together with the fact that for
i<\frac{n-1}{2}
we have

\frac{n-7+2i}{2}-(n-1)<-3\,,

justifies the last condition in the
n
-odd case of (
A.29
). It follows from (
A.32
) that the coefficients in the next-to-last line of (
A.41
) are given respectively by

\displaystyle\overset{(i)}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)\,\mathcal{K}_{[\alpha]}(\tfrac{n-7-2i}{2})

\displaystyle=\overset{(i-1)}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)\,\mathcal{K}\left(\tfrac{n-5-2i}{2},\mathring{\Delta},P\right)\mathcal{K}_{[\alpha]}(\tfrac{n-7-2i}{2})\,,

(A.42)

\displaystyle\overset{(i)}{\psi}_{[\alpha]}\mathcal{K}\left(\tfrac{n-7+2i}{2},\mathring{\Delta},P\right)

\displaystyle=\overset{(i-1)}{\psi}_{[\alpha]}\mathcal{K}_{[\alpha]}(\tfrac{n-9+2i}{2})\mathcal{K}\left(\tfrac{n-7+2i}{2},\mathring{\Delta},P\right)\,.

(A.43)

Next, it can be verified using (
A.8
) that we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}\left(\tfrac{n-5-2i}{2},\mathring{\Delta},P\right)\mathcal{K}_{[\alpha]}(\tfrac{n-7-2i}{2})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{K}\left(\tfrac{n-7+2i}{2},\mathring{\Delta},P\right)\mathcal{K}_{[\alpha]}(\tfrac{n-9+2i}{2})

\displaystyle=:\mathcal{\widehat{K}}(2i,P)\,,

(A.44)

and hence

\displaystyle\overset{(i)}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)\,\alpha^{2}\mathcal{K}_{[\alpha]}(\tfrac{n-7-2i}{2})r^{(n-5-2i)/2}h_{AB}+\alpha^{2i}\overset{(i)}{\psi}_{[\alpha]}\mathcal{K}\left(\tfrac{n-7+2i}{2},\mathring{\Delta},P\right)r^{(n-9+2i)/2}h_{AB}

\displaystyle=\alpha^{2}\mathcal{\widehat{K}}(2i,P)\bigg[\overset{(i-1)}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)\,r^{(n-5-2i)/2}h_{AB}+\alpha^{2(i-1)}\overset{(i-1)}{\psi}_{[\alpha]}r^{(n-9+2i)/2}h_{AB}\bigg]

\displaystyle=\alpha^{2}\mathcal{\widehat{K}}(2i,P)\bigg[\partial_{r}\overset{(i-1)}{q}_{AB}-m^{i-1}\overset{(i-1)}{\psi}_{[m]}r^{\frac{n-7-2(i-1)(n-1)}{2}}h_{AB}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad-\sum_{j,\ell}^{(i-1)_{**}}m^{j}\alpha^{2\ell}\overset{(i-1)}{\psi}_{j,\ell}(\mathring{\Delta},P)\,r^{\frac{n-5}{2}-i-j(n-2)+2\ell}h_{AB}\bigg]\,.

(A.45)

Substituting (
A.45
) back into (
A.41
) gives an equation of the form

\displaystyle\partial_{r}\big[\partial_{u}\overset{(i)}{q}_{AB}-\overset{(i)}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)\,\hat{q}_{AB}^{(n-7-2i)/2}-\alpha^{2i}\overset{(i)}{\psi}_{[\alpha]}\hat{q}_{AB}^{(n-7+2i)/2}-m^{i}\overset{(i)}{\psi}_{[m]}\hat{q}^{(\frac{n-7-2i(n-1)}{2})}_{AB}

\displaystyle\qquad-\sum_{j,\ell}^{(i-1)_{**}}m^{j}\alpha^{2\ell}\overset{(i-1)}{\psi}_{j,\ell}(\mathring{\Delta},P)\,\hat{q}^{(\frac{1}{2}(n-7-2(i-1)-2j(n-2)+4\ell))}_{AB}-\alpha^{2}\mathcal{\widehat{K}}(2i,P)\overset{(i-1)}{q}_{AB}\big]

\displaystyle=\overset{(i+1)}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)\,r^{(n-9-2i)/2}h_{AB}+\alpha^{2(i+1)}\overset{(i+1)}{\psi}_{[\alpha]}r^{(n-5+2i)/2}h_{AB}+m^{i+1}\overset{(i+1)}{\psi}_{[m]}r^{(n-7-2(i+1)(n-1))/2}h_{AB}

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{j,\ell}^{(i+1)_{**}}m^{j}\alpha^{2\ell}\overset{(i+1)}{\psi}_{j,\ell}(\mathring{\Delta},P)\,r^{\frac{n-7}{2}-(i+1)-j(n-2)+2\ell}h_{AB}\,,

(A.46)

which establishes (
A.28
) with
i
replaced by
i+1
. This completes the induction for
n
even, or for
n
odd and
i\leq\frac{n-1}{2}
.

n
odd,
i>\frac{n-1}{2}
.

When
i=\frac{n-1}{2}
(
A.28
) reads

\displaystyle\partial_{r}\overset{(\frac{n-1}{2})}{q_{AB}}

\displaystyle=\overset{(\frac{n-1}{2})}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)\,r^{-3}h_{AB}+\alpha^{n-1}\overset{(\frac{n-1}{2})}{\psi_{\alpha}}r^{n-4}h_{AB}+m^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\overset{(\frac{n-1}{2})}{\psi}_{[m]}r^{\frac{n-7-(n-1)^{2}}{2}}h_{AB}

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{j,\ell}^{(\frac{n-1}{2})_{**}}m^{j}\alpha^{2\ell}\overset{(\frac{n-1}{2})}{\psi}_{j,\ell}(\mathring{\Delta},P)\,r^{2\ell-3-j(n-2)}h_{AB}\,.

(A.47)

For odd
n
one can now continue as follows: Taking the
u
-derivative (
A.47
) and making use of (
A.15
) and (
A.44
)-(
A.45
) with
i=\frac{n-1}{2}
gives,

\displaystyle\partial_{r}\big[\partial_{u}\overset{(\frac{n-1}{2})}{q_{AB}}-\overset{(\frac{n-1}{2})}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)\,\hat{q}^{(-3)}_{AB}-\alpha^{n-1}\overset{(\frac{n-1}{2})}{\psi_{\alpha}}\hat{q}_{AB}^{(n-4)}-\alpha^{2}\mathcal{\widehat{K}}(n-1,P)\hat{q}_{AB}^{\frac{n-3}{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad-m^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\overset{(\frac{n-1}{2})}{\psi}_{[m]}\hat{q}^{(\frac{n-7-(n-1)^{2}}{2})}-\sum_{j,\ell}^{(\frac{n-1}{2})_{**}}m^{j}\alpha^{2\ell}\overset{(\frac{n-1}{2})}{\psi}_{j,\ell}(\mathring{\Delta},P)\,\hat{q}_{AB}^{(2\ell-3-j(n-2))}\big]

\displaystyle=\overset{(\frac{n-1}{2})}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)\,\big[\big(\mathring{\mathcal{K}}\left(-3,\mathring{\Delta},P\right)-\frac{2}{n}\log{(\tfrac{r}{r_{2}})}P\big)r^{-4}+m\,\mathcal{K}_{[m]}(-3)r^{-2-n}\big]h_{AB}

\displaystyle\quad+\alpha^{n-1}\overset{(\frac{n-1}{2})}{\psi_{\alpha}}\big[\alpha^{2}\underbrace{\mathcal{K}_{[\alpha]}(n-4)}_{=0}r^{n-3}+m\underbrace{\mathcal{K}_{[m]}(n-4)}_{=0}r^{-3}\big]h_{AB}

\displaystyle\quad-\alpha^{2}\mathcal{\widehat{K}}(n-1,P)\sum_{j,\ell}^{(\frac{n-3}{2})_{**}}m^{j}\alpha^{2\ell}\overset{(\frac{n-3}{2})}{\psi}_{j,\ell}(\mathring{\Delta},P)\,r^{2(\ell-1)-j(n-2)}h_{AB}

\displaystyle\quad+m^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\overset{(\frac{n-1}{2})}{\psi}_{[m]}\bigg[\mathcal{K}\left(\tfrac{n-7-(n-1)^{2}}{2},\mathring{\Delta},P\right)r^{\frac{n-9-(n-1)^{2}}{2}}+\alpha^{2}\mathcal{K}_{[\alpha]}(\tfrac{n-7-(n-1)^{2}}{2})r^{\frac{n-5-(n-1)^{2}}{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+m\mathcal{K}_{[m]}(\tfrac{n-7-(n-1)^{2}}{2})r^{\frac{n-7-(n+1)(n-1)}{2}}\bigg]h_{AB}

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{j,\ell}^{(\frac{n-1}{2})_{**}}m^{j}\alpha^{2\ell}\overset{(\frac{n-1}{2})}{\psi}_{j,\ell}(\mathring{\Delta},P)\,\bigg[\mathcal{K}\left(2\ell-3-j(n-2),\mathring{\Delta},P\right)r^{2\ell-4-j(n-2)}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\alpha^{2}\mathcal{K}_{[\alpha]}(2\ell-3-j(n-2))r^{2\ell-2-j(n-2)}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+m\mathcal{K}_{[m]}(2\ell-3-j(n-2))r^{2\ell-2-j(n-2)-n}\bigg]h_{AB}

\displaystyle=\bigg[\underbrace{\overset{(\frac{n-1}{2})}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)\,\mathring{\mathcal{K}}\left(-3,\mathring{\Delta},P\right)}_{=:\overset{((n+1)/2)}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)}-\frac{2}{n}\log{(\tfrac{r}{r_{2}})}\underbrace{\overset{(\frac{n-1}{2})}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)\circ P}_{=0}\bigg]r^{-4}h_{AB}

\displaystyle\quad+m^{(n+1)/2}\overset{((n+1)/2)}{\psi}_{[m]}r^{\frac{n-n^{2}-6}{2}}h_{AB}+\sum_{j,\ell}^{(i+1)_{**}}m^{j}\alpha^{2\ell}\overset{((n+1)/2)}{\psi}_{j,\ell}(\mathring{\Delta},P)\,r^{2(j+\ell-2)-jn}h_{AB}\,,

(A.48)

where we show in Appendix
C.5
, p.
C.5

that the “
\log
-term operator” in the right-hand side of the last equality is zero when acting on symmetric traceless tensors. More precisely, for any vector field
X
we have

\displaystyle\overset{(\frac{n-1}{2})}{\psi}

\displaystyle(\mathring{\Delta},P)\circ\,C(X)=0\,\,\implies\,\,

\displaystyle\overset{(\frac{n-1}{2})}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)\circ P(h)=\overset{(\frac{n-1}{2})}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)\circ\underbrace{P\big(h^{[{\mathrm{TT}}]}}_{\equiv 0}+C(X)\big)=0\,.

(A.49)

This also implies that
all logarithmic terms in
\hat{q}^{(-3)}_{AB}
(cf. (
A
)) appearing in the first line of (
A.48
) vanish, since they are always multiplied by
P(h)_{AB}
or
C(h_{uA})
.

Putting this together, we have

\displaystyle\partial_{r}\overset{((n+1)/2)}{q_{AB}}

\displaystyle=\overset{((n+1)/2)}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)\,r^{-4}h_{AB}+m^{(n+1)/2}\overset{((n+1)/2)}{\psi}_{[m]}r^{\frac{n-n^{2}-6}{2}}h_{AB}

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{j,\ell}^{i_{**}}m^{j}\alpha^{2\ell}\overset{((n+1)/2)}{\psi}_{j,\ell}(\mathring{\Delta},P)\,r^{2(j+\ell-2)-jn}h_{AB}\,,

(A.50)

where
\overset{((n+1)/2)}{q_{AB}}
is given by the term in the square brackets of the first line of (
A.48
).

For
i>(n+1)/2
, the recursion continues as before, with
\overset{(i)}{q}_{AB}
now given by (
A.37
).
Note that, the only difference in the structure of this expression compared to (
A.35
) is that the
\alpha
terms which are not multiplied by factors of
m
vanish due to the vanishing of
\mathcal{K}_{[\alpha]}(n-4)
and
\mathcal{K}_{[\alpha]}(-4)
.
The recursion again follows by induction and the calculation is exactly the same as in (
A.41
).

We also note that when
k\neq-3
, we have under gauge transformations,

\displaystyle\hat{q}_{AB}^{(k)}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\hat{q}_{AB}^{(k)}-\frac{2}{(7+2k-n)(3+k)}

\displaystyle\phantom{\rightarrow\hat{q}_{AB}^{k}}\times\bigg[r^{k+1}\bigg(\frac{(n-1)(k-n+5)}{(n-2)(k-n+3)}P+(k+3)(k-n+5){\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\phantom{\rightarrow\hat{q}_{AB}^{k}\times\bigg[r^{k+1}\bigg(}-{\frac{2(k+3)(k-n+4)^{2}}{k-n+3}mr^{2-n}}-(k+4)(k-n+2)\alpha^{2}r^{2}\bigg)\mathop{\mathrm{TS}}[{\mathring{D}}_{A}{\mathring{D}}_{B}\xi^{u}]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\quad-r^{k+2}\bigg(\left(\frac{2}{k-n+3}+2\right)P-(k+3)(k-n+4)\left(\alpha^{2}r^{2}+{2mr^{2-n}}-{\varepsilon}\right)\bigg)C(\xi)_{AB}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\quad+r^{k+3}(n-7-2k)C(\partial_{u}\xi)_{AB}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\quad+r^{k+2}\bigg(\frac{2(k+3)}{n-1}\bigg)\mathop{\mathrm{TS}}[{\mathring{D}}_{A}{\mathring{D}}_{B}{\mathring{D}}_{C}\xi^{C}]\bigg]\,.

(A.51)

When
k=-3
,
m=0
, the
r-
independent term from the gauge transformation of
\hat{q}^{(-3)}_{AB}
reads

\displaystyle\hat{q}_{AB}^{(-3)}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\hat{q}_{AB}^{(-3)}+\left(n-\frac{2}{n}-\frac{2}{n-1}\right)C(\partial_{u}\xi)_{AB}+\frac{(n(n+2)-2)}{n}\alpha^{2}\mathop{\mathrm{TS}}[{\mathring{D}}_{A}{\mathring{D}}_{B}\xi^{u}]+\ldots\,,

(A.52)

where the
(\ldots)
above contains
r
-dependent terms.

A.3
The case
n=3

As mentioned in the introduction, we provide here the complete list of gauge-invariant obstructions to gluing in spacetime dimension four, as some of the obstructions listed in
ChCong1
were not gauge-invariant. For this, we reexamine our analysis so far with
n=3
.

When
n=3
, the recursion relation, in Appendix
A.1
here, for
\overset{(i)}{H}_{uA}
remains unchanged. However, that for the field
\overset{(i)}{q}_{AB}
simplifies due to the vanishing of the operator

\displaystyle P-\frac{1}{2}(\mathring{\Delta}+2\mathring{\mathscr{R}})

(A.53)

acting on symmetric trace-free tensors, which follows from (
3.95
) after noting that
\mathop{\mathrm{TS}}[R[\gamma]_{AB}]=0
when
n=3
. Thus, when
n=3
and
i=\frac{n-1}{2}=1
we have (compare also (
A.28
) with
(
ChCong1
, Equation (101))
),

\displaystyle\overset{(\frac{n-1}{2})}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)=\overset{(1)}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)=-\frac{4}{n+1}P+\frac{1}{2}(\mathring{\Delta}+2\mathring{\mathscr{R}})=0\,.

(A.54)

As a result, the equations
\mathop{\mathrm{TS}}[\partial_{u}^{i-1}\mycal E_{AB}]=0
coincide with (
A.28
) where the first and last term vanish, i.e.,

\displaystyle\forall i\in\mathbbm{Z}^{+}\,,\quad\partial_{r}\overset{(i)}{q}_{AB}

\displaystyle=\alpha^{2i}\overset{(i)}{\psi}_{[\alpha]}r^{(n-7+2i)/2}h_{AB}+m^{i}\overset{(i)}{\psi}_{[m]}r^{\frac{n-7-2i(n-1)}{2}}h_{AB}\,.

(A.55)

In the rest of this appendix, for ease of comparison we typically keep
n
in the equations but
we assume
n=3
unless explicitly indicated otherwise.

One verifies that the recursion formulae (
A.31
)-(
A.32
) for
\overset{(i)}{\psi}_{[m]}
and
\overset{(i)}{\psi}_{[\alpha]}
remain unchanged.

Moreover, it follows from arguments identical to those leading to (
A.36
) that we have, for positive integers
i
,

\displaystyle\overset{(i)}{\psi}_{[\alpha]}=\begin{cases}1\,,&i=1\,,\\
0\,,&\text{otherwise}\,.\end{cases}

(A.56)

With some further work one finds that the recursion formula (
A.35
) for
\overset{(i)}{q}_{AB}
is now given by

\displaystyle\overset{(i)}{q}_{AB}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\partial_{u}\overset{(1)}{q}_{AB}-\alpha^{2}\hat{q}^{(\frac{n-5}{2})}_{AB}-m\hat{q}^{(\frac{n-7-2(n-1)}{2})}_{AB}\,,&i=2\\
\partial_{u}\overset{(i-1)}{q}_{AB}-m^{i-1}\overset{(i-1)}{\psi}_{[m]}\hat{q}^{(\frac{n-7-4(i-1)}{2})}_{AB}\,,&i>2\,.\end{cases}

(A.57)

Radial charges.

When
n=3
, the obstructions in Tables
1.1
-
1.2
for
m\neq 0
remain as they are. When
m=0
, which we will assume for the rest of this appendix, most of the obstructions listed in Table
1.3
remain as presented, with terms in the obstructions involving fields
\overset{(p)}{q}_{AB}
for
p\leq\frac{n-3}{2}=0
understood to be vacuous. This includes the radial charge
\overset{[3]}{Q}
of (
4.35
), p.
4.35
, which we recall was defined, when
n>3
, as

\displaystyle\overset{[3]}{Q}:=\!\operatorname{\mathring{\,L}}\big(\overset{(\frac{n-3}{2})}{q}{}^{[\ker\overset{(\frac{n-3}{2})}{\psi}]})+\alpha^{n-3}\frac{n-1}{n-3}\overset{(\frac{n-3}{2})}{\psi}_{[\alpha]}\chi\,,\quad n>3\,.

(A.58)

When
n=3
, it is convenient to define
\overset{[3]}{Q}
instead as the radial (but
not
gauge-invariant)
charge
\chi
:

\displaystyle\overset{[3]}{Q}:=\chi\,,\quad n=3\,.

(A.59)

The gauge transformation of
{\mathring{D}}_{A}\overset{[3]}{Q}
(cf. (
3.3.3
) with
n=3
) will play a role in the definition of
\overset{[5,i]}{Q}
below (cf. (
D.13
) and following):

\displaystyle{\mathring{D}}_{A}\overset{[3]}{Q}\rightarrow{\mathring{D}}_{A}\overset{[3]}{Q}-\frac{1}{2}(\mathring{\Delta}-{{\varepsilon}})(\mathring{\Delta}+{{\varepsilon}}){\mathring{D}}_{A}\xi^{u}\,.

(A.60)

In addition, the expression (
4.51
) for the operator
\operatorname{L_{n}}
needs to be modified, since
\overset{(\frac{n-5}{2})}{\psi}
is clearly undefined when
n=3
.
In analogy with the higher dimensional case, we will define the operator
\operatorname{L}_{3}
to be that associated to the gauge transformation of
\overset{(\frac{n-1}{2})}{q}_{AB}
, equivalently
q_{AB}
for
n=3
, when
\alpha=m=0
, which by (
3.101
) reads

\displaystyle q_{AB}\rightarrow\

\displaystyle q_{AB}\underbrace{-\big(\mathop{\mathrm{TS}}[{\mathring{D}}_{A}{\mathring{D}}_{B}{\mathring{D}}_{C}\xi^{C}]-\left(P-{\varepsilon}\right)C(\xi)_{AB}\big)}_{=:\operatorname{L}_{3}(\xi)_{AB}}\,.

(A.61)

Making use of (
6.4.3
) with
a=1
,
b=c=0
, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(2)}}\circ\operatorname{L}_{3}=\pm\frac{1}{4}(\mathring{\Delta}-{{\varepsilon}})(\mathring{\Delta}+{{\varepsilon}})\,,

(A.62)

where the plus sign is for
V
and the minus sign is for
S
.
Note that this is half of the operator acting on
{\mathring{D}}_{A}\xi^{u}
in the gauge transformation of
{\mathring{D}}_{A}\overset{[3]}{Q}
in (
A.60
), consistently with the
n>3
result in
(
4.45
), using (
D.9
) below.

The radial charge
\overset{[4]}{Q}
is defined as in (
4.37
), and its gauge transformation remains that of (
4.53
):

\displaystyle\overset{[4]}{Q}\rightarrow\overset{[4]}{Q}+\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(2)}}\circ\operatorname{L}_{3}(\xi)\,.

(A.63)

Finally, a direct calculation of the gauge transformation of the field
{\mathring{D}}^{B}\overset{(2)}{q}_{AB}
using (
A.51
) gives

\displaystyle{\mathring{D}}^{B}\overset{(2)}{q}_{AB}\rightarrow{\mathring{D}}^{B}\overset{(2)}{q}_{AB}+{\mathring{D}}^{B}\operatorname{L}_{3}(\partial_{u}\xi)_{AB}+\alpha^{2}{\mathring{D}}^{B}\operatorname{L}_{3}({\mathring{D}}\xi^{u})_{AB}\,.

(A.64)

Remark
A.1
.

The additional
\alpha^{2}
-term in this gauge transformation was not present in the higher dimensional case (
4.54
) (the gauge transformation of the projection
\overset{(p)}{q}{}_{AB}^{[\ker\overset{(p)}{\psi}]}
is given there; however since
\overset{(p)}{\psi}=0
for all integers
p\geq 1
, the projection
\overset{(p)}{q}{}_{AB}^{[\ker\overset{(p)}{\psi}]}
coincides with
\overset{(p)}{q}_{AB}
when
n=3
, and therefore the transformation law (
4.54
) should be read after removing the integral against the
\mu
-field). This additional term is sourced by the field
\alpha^{2}\hat{q}_{AB}^{(\frac{n-5}{2})}
appearing in
\overset{(2)}{q}_{AB}
of (
A.57
).
The analogous term in dimensions
n>3
is the term
\alpha^{n-1}\overset{(\frac{n-1}{2})}{\psi}_{[\alpha]}\hat{q}_{AB}^{(\frac{n-5}{2})}
appearing in (
A.35
)
with
i=\frac{n+1}{2}
.
Now,
both
the field
\overset{(\frac{n+1}{2})}{q}_{AB}
and
\alpha

have the same length dimension as
r^{-1}
, while the gauge field
\xi^{u}
has the same dimension as
r
, and
\xi^{A}
is dimensionless.
Thus, the only combination of
\alpha
and gauge fields that is possible in the gauge transformation of
\overset{(\frac{n+1}{2})}{q}{}^{[\ker\overset{(\frac{n+1}{2})}{\psi}]}_{AB}
are
\partial_{u}\xi^{A}
and
\alpha^{2}\xi^{u}
. A term of the latter form is only possible in
\alpha^{n-1}\overset{(\frac{n-1}{2})}{\psi}_{[\alpha]}\hat{q}_{AB}^{(\frac{n-5}{2})}
when
n-1=2
, i.e.,
n=3
, thus explaining the missing contributions of this term in higher dimensions.

\Box

It follows inductively from (
A.57
) and (
A.64
) that for integers
i>2
, under gauge transformations respecting (
3.28
),

\displaystyle{\mathring{D}}^{B}\overset{(i)}{q}_{AB}\rightarrow{\mathring{D}}^{B}\overset{(i)}{q}_{AB}+{\mathring{D}}^{B}\operatorname{L}_{3}(\partial^{i-1}_{u}\xi)_{AB}+\frac{\alpha^{2}}{2}{\mathring{D}}^{B}\operatorname{L}_{3}({\mathring{D}}\circ\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(1)}}\partial_{u}^{i-3}\xi)_{AB}\,.

(A.65)

Appendix B
Some mapping properties

In this section we will verify equation (
5.20
) for various operators which arise in the gluing construction.

B.1
Elliptic operators

Consider an elliptic operator
\operatorname{L}
of order
\ell
, with smooth coefficients, on a compact boundaryless
d
-dimensional manifold
{}^{d}M
. We will write
\operatorname{L}|_{X}
to denote the restriction of
\operatorname{L}
to a subspace
X\subset H^{\ell}
, and
\operatorname{L}^{\dagger}
for the formal adjoint of
\operatorname{L}
obtained by integration by parts against smooth functions; note that no boundary terms arise in the current case.
We wish to verify a more precise version of (
5.20
), namely:

Proposition
B.1
.

Under the conditions just stated, for
k\geq 0
it holds that

(\ker\operatorname{L}^{\dagger})^{\perp}\cap H^{k}({{}^{d}M})=\text{im}\,(\operatorname{L}|_{H^{\ell+k}({{}^{d}M})})\,.

(B.1)

Equivalently, we have the
L^{2}
-orthogonal splitting

H^{k}({{}^{d}M})=\ker\operatorname{L}^{\dagger}\oplus\text{im}\,(\operatorname{L}|_{H^{\ell+k}({{}^{d}M})})\,.

(B.2)

Here, and elsewhere, orthogonality is meant in
L^{2}
, in particular
(\ker\operatorname{L}^{\dagger})^{\perp}
is a subspace of
L^{2}
.

Before passing to the proof, let us recall a few standard properties of elliptic operators. Letting
\|\cdot\|_{k}
denote the
H^{k}
-norm, it holds that:

1.

If
\operatorname{L}\phi\in H^{k}
in a distributional sense, then
\phi\in H^{\ell+k}
and there exists a constant
C
such that we have

\|\phi\|_{\ell+k}\leq C\big(\|\operatorname{L}\phi\|_{k}+\|\phi\|_{0}\big)\,.

(B.3)

Note that this immediately implies that elements of the kernel of
\operatorname{L}
are smooth, in particular
\ker\operatorname{L}|_{H^{\ell+k}}=\ker\operatorname{L}|_{H^{\ell}}
for any
k\geq 0
.
Similarly
\ker\operatorname{L}^{\dagger}|_{H^{\ell+k}}
is independent of
k\geq 0
under the current hypotheses, and we will simply write
\ker\operatorname{L}^{\dagger}
for
\ker\operatorname{L}^{\dagger}|_{H^{\ell+k}}
, except when the indication of the domain is relevant for clarity.

2.

The spaces
\ker\operatorname{L}
and
\ker\operatorname{L}^{\dagger}
are finite dimensional.

3.

Let a function
\phi\in L^{2}
satisfy
\operatorname{L}\phi\in H^{k}
in a distributional sense.
There exists a constant
C^{\prime}
such that if
\phi
is
L^{2}
-orthogonal to
\ker\operatorname{L}
then

\|\phi\|_{\ell+k}\leq C^{\prime}\|\operatorname{L}\phi\|_{k}\,.

(B.4)

We are ready now to pass to the

Proof of Proposition
B.1
:

To prove (
B.1
) we consider,
first, a function
\phi\in L^{2}
which is orthogonal to the image of
\operatorname{L}|_{H^{\ell+k}}
, thus

\forall\psi\in H^{\ell+k}\qquad\int_{{}^{d}M}\phi\,\operatorname{L}\psi=0\,.

(B.5)

By density of
H^{\ell+k}
in
H^{\ell}
it also holds that

\forall\psi\in H^{\ell}\qquad\int_{{}^{d}M}\phi\,\operatorname{L}\psi=0\,.

(B.6)

Then
\phi
is a weak solution of the elliptic equation
\operatorname{L}^{\dagger}\phi=0
. The operator
\operatorname{L}^{\dagger}
is also elliptic, and so by elliptic regularity
\phi\in C^{\infty}
and
\operatorname{L}^{\dagger}\phi=0
in the classical sense, thus
\phi\in\ker\operatorname{L}^{\dagger}
. So

\displaystyle(\text{im}\,\operatorname{L}|_{H^{\ell+k}})^{\perp}

\displaystyle=\ker(\operatorname{L}^{\dagger}|_{H^{\ell}})\quad\Longrightarrow\quad

\displaystyle\big(\ker(\operatorname{L}^{\dagger}|_{H^{\ell}})\big)^{\perp}=\big((\text{im}\,\operatorname{L}|_{H^{\ell+k}})^{\perp}\big)^{\perp}=\overline{\text{im}\,\operatorname{L}|_{H^{\ell+k}}}\,,

(B.7)

where
\overline{\text{im}\,\operatorname{L}|_{H^{\ell+k}}}
is the closure of
\text{im}\,\operatorname{L}|_{H^{\ell+k}}
in
L^{2}
.
We will verify that
\text{im}\,\operatorname{L}|_{H^{\ell}}
is closed in
L^{2}
, and hence (
B.1
) holds with
k=0
:

\text{im}\,\operatorname{L}|_{H^{\ell}}=\big(\ker(\operatorname{L}^{\dagger}|_{H^{\ell}})\big)^{\perp}\,.

(B.8)

(Note that
\text{im}\,\operatorname{L}|_{H^{\ell+k}}
is not closed in
L^{2}
in general, so that from (
B.7
) we can only conclude that for
k>0
we have

\displaystyle\big(\ker(\operatorname{L}^{\dagger}|_{H^{\ell}})\big)^{\perp}\supset\text{im}\,\operatorname{L}|_{H^{\ell+k}}\,;

(B.9)

however, equality suitably understood will follow from elliptic regularity.)

Now, by ellipticity the kernel
\ker\operatorname{L}|_{H^{\ell}}
is finite dimensional and therefore
\ker\operatorname{L}|_{H^{\ell}}
is closed (cf. e.g.
(
Weidmann
, Theorem 3.4)
) in
L^{2}
. Since
A^{\perp}
is closed in a Hilbert space for any set
A
, the set
(\ker\operatorname{L}|_{H^{\ell}})^{\perp}
is also closed in
L^{2}
. Therefore we have the splitting
L^{2}=\ker\operatorname{L}|_{H^{\ell}}\oplus(\ker\operatorname{L}|_{H^{\ell}})^{\perp}

(
Weidmann
, Section 3.1)
.
Hence the map

\operatorname{L}|_{H^{\ell}}:(\ker\operatorname{L}|_{H^{\ell}})^{\perp}\to\text{im}\,\operatorname{L}|_{H^{\ell}}

is surjective.

Consider a sequence
\psi_{n}
in
\text{im}\,\operatorname{L}|_{H^{\ell}}
converging in
L^{2}
to
\psi
. We have
\psi_{n}=\operatorname{L}\phi_{n}
for a sequence
\phi_{n}\in H^{\ell}\cap(\ker\operatorname{L}|_{H^{\ell}})^{\perp}
.
Since
\psi_{n}
is Cauchy in
L^{2}
, linearity together with (
B.4
) shows that the sequence
\phi_{n}
is Cauchy in
H^{\ell}
, and therefore there exists
\phi
such that
\phi_{n}\to\phi
in
H^{\ell}
. By continuity
\psi=\operatorname{L}\phi
, and
\text{im}\,\operatorname{L}|_{H^{\ell}}
is closed, as claimed.

To finish the proof, consider
\psi\in(\ker\operatorname{L}^{\dagger})^{\perp}\cap H^{k}
with
k>0
. By (
B.8
) we have
\psi\in\text{im}\,\operatorname{L}|_{H^{\ell}}
, thus
\psi=\operatorname{L}\phi
for some
\phi\in H^{\ell}
. By elliptic regularity
\phi\in H^{\ell+k}
, and so
\psi\in\text{im}\,\operatorname{L}|_{H^{\ell+k}}
, as desired.

\Box

Remark
B.2
.

Identical arguments apply mutatis mutandi in
Hölder spaces
C^{k,\lambda}({}^{d}M)
for any
\lambda\in(0,1)
, and in Sobolev spaces
W^{k,p}({}^{d}M)
for any
p\in(1,\infty)
, and in
L^{2}
-type Sobolev spaces
H^{\ell+s}({}^{d}M)
for any
s\in[0,\infty)
, and in fact in any spaces in which the equivalents of the inequalities (
B.3
)-(
B.4
) hold.

B.2
\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(1)}}

Proposition
B.3
.

For
k\geq 0
we have

\text{im}\big(\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(1)}}|_{H^{k+1}({{}^{d}M})}\big)=\big(\ker\operatorname{(\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(1)})^{\dagger}}\big)^{\perp}\cap H^{k}({{}^{d}M})\,.

Proof.

For all
\xi\in H^{k+1}
we have

\int\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(1)}}\xi=0\,,

hence

\text{im}\big(\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(1)}}|_{H^{k+1}}\big)\subset\{1\}^{\perp}=\big(\ker\operatorname{(\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(1)})^{\dagger}})^{\perp}\,.

For the reverse inclusion, note that for every
\psi\in\{1\}^{\perp}\cap H^{k}
there exists
\phi\in H^{k+2}
such that
\Delta\phi=\psi
. Setting
\xi={\mathring{D}}\phi\in H^{k+1}
one obtains
\psi=\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(1)}}\xi
, thus

\text{im}\big(\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(1)}}|_{H^{k+1}}\big)\supset\{1\}^{\perp}\cap H^{k}\,.

B.3
\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(2)}}

While this is irrelevant for our purposes here, we note that
\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(2)}}
(acting on symmetric traceless two-tensors) is conformally covariant in all dimensions. In particular, in dimension
{d}\geq 2
, if
g_{AB}=\phi^{-\ell}\bar{g}_{AB}
then

D_{A}h^{AB}=\phi^{({d}+2)\ell/2}\bar{D}_{A}(\phi^{-({d}+2)\ell/2}h^{AB})\,,

(B.10)

where
D
is the Levi-Civita connection of
g
and
\bar{D}
that of
\bar{g}
.
This shows that it suffices to understand the kernel of
\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(2)}}
for, e.g., metrics of constant scalar curvature.

In any case we have:

Proposition
B.4
.

For
k\geq 0
it holds that

\text{im}\big(\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(2)}}|_{H^{k+1}({{}^{d}M})}\big)={\mathrm{CKV}^{\perp}}\cap H^{k}({{}^{d}M})\,.

In particular if
\mathring{R}_{BC}<0
, then the operator
{}\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(2)}}{}
is surjective.

Proof.

For all conformal Killing vectors
\xi
and all symmetric traceless tensors
h\in H^{1}
we have

\int\xi^{A}\mathring{D}^{B}h_{AB}=-\int\mathring{D}^{B}\xi^{A}h_{AB}=-\int\mathop{\mathrm{TS}}(\mathring{D}^{B}\xi^{A})h_{AB}=0\,.

(B.11)

Thus

\text{im}\big(\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(2)}}|_{H^{k+1}}\big)\subset{\mathrm{CKV}^{\perp}}\cap H^{k}\,.

The reverse inclusion follows as in the proof of Proposition
B.3
using the elliptic operator
\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(2)}}\circ\,C
instead of the Laplacian.

Since the space of conformal Killing vectors is trivial when the Ricci tensor is negative by Proposition
C.1
below,
surjectivity for such metrics follows.

\Box

B.4
\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(1)}}\circ\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(2)}}

Proposition
B.5
.

Suppose that
({}^{d}M,{\mathring{\gamma}})
is Einstein.
For
k\geq 2
it holds that

\text{im}\big(\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(1)}}\circ\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(2)}}|_{H^{k+2}({{}^{d}M})}\big)=\Big(\ker\big((\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(1)}}\circ\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(2)}})^{\dagger}\big)\Big)^{\perp}\cap H^{k}({{}^{d}M})\,.

(B.12)

Remark
B.6
.

It seems clear that the hypothesis that the metric is Einstein is not necessary, but the result in this form is sufficient for our purposes.

Proof.

For all functions
\psi\in H^{2}
and for for all symmetric and traceless tensors
h\in H^{2}
we have

\int\psi\mathring{D}^{B}\mathring{D}^{A}h_{AB}=\int\mathop{\mathrm{TS}}(\mathring{D}^{A}\mathring{D}^{B}\psi)h_{AB}\,.

(B.13)

Thus

(\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(1)}}\circ\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(2)}})^{\dagger}=C\circ{\mathring{D}}\,,

(B.14)

and

\text{im}\big(\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(1)}}\circ\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(2)}}|_{H^{k+2}}\big)\subset\Big(\ker(C\circ{\mathring{D}})\Big)^{\perp}\cap H^{k}\,.

To obtain the reverse inclusion,
let
h_{AB}=C({\mathring{D}}\phi)_{AB}
for a function
\phi\in H^{k+4}
. Then

{\mathring{D}}^{A}{\mathring{D}}^{B}h_{AB}=\frac{d-1}{d}\mathring{\Delta}^{2}\phi+\frac{1}{2}({\mathring{D}}^{A}\mathring{R}){\mathring{D}}_{A}\phi+\mathring{R}_{AB}{\mathring{D}}^{A}{\mathring{D}}^{B}\phi=\frac{d-1}{d}\mathring{\Delta}(\mathring{\Delta}+{{\varepsilon}}d)\phi\,.

(B.15)

Since
d>2
, the operator at the right-hand side of this equation is an isomorphism between

\ker(C\circ{\mathring{D}})^{\perp}\cap H^{k+4}

and

\ker(C\circ{\mathring{D}})^{\perp}\cap H^{k}
,
which finishes the proof.

B.5
\operatorname{L_{n}}
and
\operatorname{\!\!\check{\,\,L}}_{n}

Recall that (cf. Remark
4.2
, p.
4.2
)

\displaystyle\operatorname{L_{n}}

\displaystyle=\overset{(\frac{n-5}{2})}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)\,\underline{\operatorname{L_{n}}}

(B.16)

\displaystyle\underline{\operatorname{L_{n}}}(\xi)_{AB}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{8}(\mathring{\Delta}+2\mathring{\mathscr{R}}-4{{\varepsilon}})(\mathring{\Delta}+2\mathring{\mathscr{R}}-6{{\varepsilon}})C(\xi)_{AB}&\\
\qquad\qquad\qquad-\frac{1}{6}(\mathring{\Delta}+2\mathring{\mathscr{R}}-5{{\varepsilon}})\mathop{\mathrm{TS}}[{\mathring{D}}_{A}{\mathring{D}}_{B}{\mathring{D}}_{C}\xi^{C}]\,,&n=5\,,\\
\frac{1}{(n-1)(n-5)}\bigg((\mathring{\Delta}+2\mathring{\mathscr{R}}-2(n-2){{\varepsilon}})(\mathring{\Delta}+2\mathring{\mathscr{R}}+(1-n){{\varepsilon}})C(\xi)_{AB}&\\
\qquad\qquad\qquad-\frac{2(n-3)}{n-2}(\mathring{\Delta}+2\mathring{\mathscr{R}}+(5-2n){{\varepsilon}})\mathop{\mathrm{TS}}[{\mathring{D}}_{A}{\mathring{D}}_{B}{\mathring{D}}_{C}\xi^{C}]\bigg)\,,&n\neq 5\,,\end{cases}

(B.17)

and (cf. (
4.47
))

\displaystyle\operatorname{\!\!\check{\,\,L}}_{n}(\xi^{u})=\begin{cases}-\left(\frac{2}{9}P-{{\varepsilon}}\right)\circ\,C({\mathring{D}}_{A}\xi^{u})\,,&n=5\\
-\frac{n-4}{(n-5)(n-2)^{2}}\overset{(\frac{n-5}{2})}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)\,\bigg((n-1)P-2(n-2)^{2}{{\varepsilon}}\bigg)\circ\,C({\mathring{D}}_{A}\xi^{u})\,,&n>5\,.\end{cases}

(B.18)

The following observation turns out to be useful for the problem at hand:

Lemma
B.7
.

Let
k\geq 0
and let
A
and
B
be linear partial differential operators of orders
a
and
b
such that
A\circ B
is elliptic.
Then

1.

\ker B
is finite dimensional with
\ker B\subset C^{\infty}
.

2.

If

\ker B=\ker(A\circ B)\,,

(B.19)

then

\text{im}\big(B|_{H^{k+a+b}({{}^{d}M})}\big)

is closed in
H^{k+a}({{}^{d}M})
.

Proof.

1. We have
\ker B\subset\ker A\circ B
, and the result follows form ellipticity.

2.
Let
\psi\in\overline{\text{im}B|_{H^{k+a+b}}}
, thus there exists a sequence
\psi_{n}\in\text{im}B|_{H^{k+a+b}}
converging to
\psi
in
H^{k+a}
. By definition there exists
\phi_{n}\in H^{k+a+b}
such that
\psi_{n}=B\phi_{n}
. Since
\ker B
is finite dimensional we have the
L^{2}
-orthogonal decomposition

H^{k+a+b}=\ker B\oplus\big((\ker B)^{\perp}\cap H^{k+a+b}\big)\,.

(B.20)

Indeed, let
\phi_{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,\dim\ker B
, be a basis of
\ker B
, then
(\ker B)^{\perp}
is the intersection of the zero-sets of the finite number of continuous functionals

H^{k+a+b}\ni\psi\mapsto\int\phi_{i}B^{\dagger}\psi\,,

and is therefore closed.

In view of (
B.19
) we can
write
\phi_{n}=\phi_{n}^{\parallel}+\phi_{n}^{\perp}
, with
\phi_{n}^{\parallel}\in\ker B
and
\phi_{n}^{\perp}\in\ker B^{\perp}
, and it holds that

\psi_{n}=B\phi_{n}^{\perp}\,.

(B.21)

The sequence
\psi_{n}\in\text{im}B|_{H^{k+a+b}}
is Cauchy in
H^{k+a}
. By continuity and linearity the sequence
A\psi_{n}
is Cauchy in
H^{k}
.
Since

(\ker B)^{\perp}=\big(\ker(A\circ B)\big)^{\perp}
by hypothesis we have
\psi_{n}\in\big(\ker(A\circ B)\big)^{\perp}
.
This shows that we can use (
B.4
) with
L=A\circ B
to conclude that the sequence

\phi_{n}^{\perp}
is Cauchy in
H^{k+a+b}
,
thus the limit
\phi=\lim_{n\to\infty}\phi_{n}^{\perp}
exists.
Continuity of
B:H^{k+a+b}\to H^{k+a}
shows that

\psi:=\lim_{n\to\infty}\psi_{n}=\lim_{n\to\infty}B\phi_{n}^{\perp}=B\phi\,,

(B.22)

and
\text{im}\,B
is a closed subspace of
H^{k+a}
, as desired.

\Box

Recall that
S
denotes the space of vector fields which are gradients, and
V
that of vector fields which have vanishing divergence. We have

Lemma
B.8
.

For
k\geq 1
the spaces

S^{k}:=S\cap H^{k}({{}^{d}M})\ \mbox{and}\ V^{k}:=V\cap H^{k}({{}^{d}M})

are closed, and we have the
L^{2}
-orthogonal splitting

H^{k}({{}^{d}M})=S^{k}\oplus V^{k}\,.

(B.23)

Proof.

V
is the kernel of the continuous operator
\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(1)}}:H^{k}\to H^{k-1}
, hence closed.

The space
S^{k}
is the image of
{\mathring{D}}|_{H^{k+1}}
. The fact that
S^{k}
is closed follows from Lemma
B.7
with
A=\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(1)}}
and
B={\mathring{D}}
, since
\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(1)}}\circ{\mathring{D}}=\mathring{\Delta}
is elliptic and the kernels of
B
and
A\circ B
coincide.

The splitting (
B.23
) follows immediately from the equality

\int\xi_{A}{\mathring{D}}^{A}\varphi=-\int\varphi{\mathring{D}}^{A}\xi_{A}\,,

(B.24)

with the last integral vanishing if
\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(1)}}\xi=0
.

\Box

Proposition
B.9
.

For
k\geq 0
and
n\geq 5
the image

\text{im}_{k}\operatorname{L_{n}}:=\text{im}\,\operatorname{L_{n}}|_{H^{k+2n}({{}^{d}M})}

is closed in
H^{k+n}({{}^{d}M})
, and we have the
L^{2}
-orthogonal splitting

H^{k+n}({{}^{d}M})=\text{im}_{k}\operatorname{L_{n}}\oplus\big(\ker\operatorname{L_{n}^{\dagger}}\cap H^{k+n}({{}^{d}M})\big)\,.

(B.25)

Proof.

For closedness it suffices to check that the operator
\operatorname{L_{n}^{\dagger}}\circ\operatorname{L_{n}}
is elliptic,
with the same kernel as
\operatorname{L_{n}}
;
the result follows then from Lemma
B.7
with
A=\operatorname{L_{n}^{\dagger}}
and
B=\operatorname{L_{n}}
.

The equality of kernels follows readily from the identity

\int|\operatorname{L_{n}}\psi|^{2}=\int\psi\,\operatorname{L_{n}^{\dagger}}\big(\operatorname{L_{n}}(\psi)\big)\,.

For ellipticity, consider those terms in
\operatorname{L_{n}^{\dagger}}
which are relevant for the determination
of its principal part. After several commutations, having discarded lower order terms, from (
B.17
) one obtains

\displaystyle\operatorname{L_{n}^{\dagger}}(h)_{A}

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle c_{n}\,E_{n}\circ\mathring{\Delta}\circ\Big(\mathring{\Delta}\circ\,C^{\dagger}(h)_{A}-\frac{2(n-3)}{n-2}{\mathring{D}}_{A}D^{B}D^{C}h_{BC}\Big)

(B.26)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle c_{n}\,E_{n}\circ\mathring{\Delta}\circ\Big(\mathring{\Delta}\circ\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(2)}}-\frac{2(n-3)}{n-2}{\mathring{D}}\,\circ\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(1)}}\circ\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(2)}}\Big)(h)_{A}\,,

with a dimension-dependent constant
c_{n}\neq 0
, and
with
E_{n}
, which is an elliptic operator of order
n-5
, arising from the principal part of
\overset{(\frac{n-5}{2})}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)
. Hence

\displaystyle\operatorname{L_{n}^{\dagger}}\circ\operatorname{L_{n}}

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle c_{n}\,E_{n}\circ\mathring{\Delta}\circ\Big(\underbrace{\mathring{\Delta}-\frac{2(n-3)}{n-2}{\mathring{D}}\,\circ\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(1)}}}_{=:(\diamond)}\Big)\circ\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(2)}}\circ\operatorname{L_{n}}\,.

(B.27)

In the proof of Proposition
C.14
, p.
C.14
below
we show
that
\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(2)}}\circ\operatorname{L_{n}}
is elliptic. It thus remains to show that
(\diamond)
is elliptic. Now, the symbol of
(\diamond)
is

(\sigma_{(\diamond)}(k)\xi)^{A}=|k|^{2}\xi^{A}-\frac{2(n-3)}{n-2}k_{B}\xi^{B}k^{A}\,,

and ellipticity readily follows for
n\neq 4
.

\Box

Similarly, for the operator
\operatorname{\!\!\check{\,\,L}}_{n}
, we have:

Proposition
B.10
.

For
k\geq 0
and
n\geq 5
the image

\text{im}_{k}\operatorname{\!\!\check{\,\,L}}_{n}:=\text{im}\,\operatorname{\!\!\check{\,\,L}}_{n}|_{H^{k+2(n-2)}({{}^{d}M})}

is closed in
H^{k+(n-2)}({{}^{d}M})
, and we have the
L^{2}
-orthogonal splitting

H^{k+(n-2)}({{}^{d}M})=\text{im}_{k}\operatorname{\!\!\check{\,\,L}}\oplus\big(\ker\operatorname{\!\!\check{\,\,L}}_{n}^{\dagger}\cap H^{k+(n-2)}({{}^{d}M})\big)\,.

(B.28)

Proof.

The proof is rather similar to that of Proposition
B.9
, we provide the details for completeness.

For closedness it suffices to check that the operator
\operatorname{\!\!\check{\,\,L}}_{n}^{\dagger}\circ\operatorname{\!\!\check{\,\,L}}_{n}
is elliptic;
the result follows then from Lemma
B.7
with
A=\operatorname{\!\!\check{\,\,L}}_{n}^{\dagger}
and
B=\operatorname{\!\!\check{\,\,L}}_{n}
.

Thus, consider those terms in
\operatorname{\!\!\check{\,\,L}}_{n}^{\dagger}
which are relevant for the determination
of its principal part. After several commutations (making use of (
C.55
)), having discarded lower order terms, from (
B.18
) one obtains

\displaystyle\operatorname{\!\!\check{\,\,L}}_{n}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle c_{n}\,\mathring{\Delta}\circ E_{n}\circ\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(1)}}\circ\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(2)}}

(B.29)

with a dimension-dependent constant
c_{n}\neq 0
,
and with
E_{n}
, which is an elliptic operator of order
n-5
, arising from the principal part of
\overset{(\frac{n-5}{2})}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)
. Hence

\displaystyle\operatorname{\!\!\check{\,\,L}}_{n}^{\dagger}\circ\operatorname{\!\!\check{\,\,L}}_{n}

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle c_{n}\,\mathring{\Delta}\circ E_{n}\circ\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(1)}}\circ\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(2)}}\circ\operatorname{\!\!\check{\,\,L}}_{n}\,,

(B.30)

is elliptic since
\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(1)}}\circ\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(2)}}\circ\operatorname{\!\!\check{\,\,L}}_{n}
is (cf. the proof of Proposition
C.13
, p.
C.13
below).

B.6
\psi

The following result will be useful:

Lemma
B.11
.

Let
\operatorname{L}
be a linear partial differential operator of order
\ell
and let
k\geq 0
. Then

1.

(\text{im}\,\operatorname{L}|_{H^{\ell+k}})^{\perp}\cap H^{\ell+k}=\ker\big(\operatorname{L}^{\dagger}|_{H^{\ell+k}}\big)\,.

(B.31)

2.

If

(a)

\text{im}\,(\operatorname{L}|_{H^{\ell+k}})^{\perp}\cap H^{k}
is
L^{2}
-dense in
\text{im}\,(\operatorname{L}|_{H^{\ell+k}})^{\perp}
,

(b)

and if
the
L^{2}
-closure of
\text{im}\,\operatorname{L}|_{H^{\ell+k}}
satisfies

\displaystyle\overline{\text{im}\,\operatorname{L}|_{H^{\ell+k}}}\cap H^{k}=\text{im}\,\operatorname{L}|_{H^{\ell+k}}\,,

(B.32)

then (
B.1
) holds.

Proof.

1. For
\phi\,,\lambda\in H^{\ell+k}
with
k\geq 0
it holds that

\int_{{}^{d}M}\phi\,\operatorname{L}\lambda=\int_{{}^{d}M}\lambda\,\operatorname{L}^{\dagger}\phi\,.

(B.33)

If
\operatorname{L}^{\dagger}\phi=0
the right-hand side is zero, which shows that

\ker\big(\operatorname{L}^{\dagger}|_{H^{\ell+k}}\big)\subset(\text{im}\,\operatorname{L}|_{H^{\ell+k}})^{\perp}\,.

(B.34)

To obtain the reverse inclusion, let
\phi\in H^{\ell+k}
be orthogonal to the image of
\operatorname{L}|_{H^{\ell+k}}
.
Then the left-hand side of (
B.33
) vanishes and we find

\forall\ \lambda\in H^{\ell+k}\qquad 0=\int_{{}^{d}M}\lambda\,\operatorname{L}^{\dagger}\phi\,.

(B.35)

By density of
H^{\ell+k}
in
L^{2}
it also holds that

\forall\ \lambda\in L^{2}\qquad\int_{{}^{d}M}\lambda\,\operatorname{L}^{\dagger}\phi=0\,.

(B.36)

Thus
\operatorname{L}^{\dagger}\phi=0
, and together with (
B.34
) we obtain (
B.31
).

2. Condition (a) together with point 1. and standard properties of orthogonality in
L^{2}
(cf., e.g.,
(
Weidmann
, Theorem 3.4)
) yield

\displaystyle\Big(\ker\big(\operatorname{L}^{\dagger}|_{H^{\ell+k}}\big)\Big)^{\perp}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big((\text{im}\,\operatorname{L}|_{H^{\ell+k}})^{\perp}\cap H^{\ell+k}\Big)^{\perp}=\Big(\overline{(\text{im}\,\operatorname{L}|_{H^{\ell+k}})^{\perp}\cap H^{\ell+k}}\Big)^{\perp}

(B.37)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big(\overline{(\text{im}\,\operatorname{L}|_{H^{\ell+k}})^{\perp}}\Big)^{\perp}=\Big((\text{im}\,\operatorname{L}|_{H^{\ell+k}})^{\perp}\Big)^{\perp}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\overline{\text{im}\,\operatorname{L}|_{H^{\ell+k}}}\,,

where “
\overline{\cdot}
” denotes closure in the
L^{2}
- topology. Hence, by hypothesis (b),

\Big(\ker\big(\operatorname{L}^{\dagger}|_{H^{\ell+k}}\big)\Big)^{\perp}\cap H^{k}=\overline{\text{im}\,\operatorname{L}|_{H^{\ell+k}}}\cap H^{k}=\text{im}\,\operatorname{L}|_{H^{\ell+k}}\,.

\Box

Proposition
B.12
.

For
k\geq 1
the image

\text{im}_{k}\overset{(i)}{\psi}:=\text{im}\big(\overset{(i)}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)|_{H^{k+2i}({}^{d}M)}\big)

is closed in
H^{k}({{}^{d}M})
, and we have

\displaystyle\text{im}_{k}\overset{(i)}{\psi}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big(\ker\overset{(i)}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)\big)^{\perp}\cap H^{k}({{}^{d}M})\,.

(B.38)

Proof.

We start by noting that the operators
\overset{(i)}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)
, which we will denote by
\overset{(i)}{\psi}
for brevity, are formally self-adjoint, of order
2i
.
When
i<\frac{n-3}{2}
, or when the pair
(n,\ell)
is convenient and
i\in\mathbb{N}
, the
\overset{(i)}{\psi}
’s are elliptic by Propositions
C.10
and
C.12
below.
Our claim follows then from Proposition
B.1
.

To continue, let the pair
(n,\ell)
be inconvenient and let
i=\frac{n-3}{2}
.
We have the
L^{2}
-orthogonal splittings

\displaystyle(V\oplus{\mathrm{TT}})\cap H^{k+n-3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ker\big(\overset{(\frac{n-3}{2})}{\psi}|_{V\oplus{\mathrm{TT}}}\big)\oplus\Big(\underbrace{\Big(\ker\big(\overset{(\frac{n-3}{2})}{\psi}|_{V\oplus{\mathrm{TT}}}\big)\Big)^{\perp}\cap H^{k+n-3}}_{=:X^{k+n-3}}\Big)\,,

(B.39)

\displaystyle H^{k+n-3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underbrace{\big(S\cap H^{k+n-3}\big)\oplus\ker\big(\overset{(\frac{n-3}{2})}{\psi}|_{V\oplus{\mathrm{TT}}}\big)}_{=\ker\overset{(\frac{n-3}{2})}{\psi}|_{H^{k+n-3}}}\oplus X^{k+n-3}\,.

(B.40)

We claim that the operators

\overset{(\frac{n-3}{2})}{\psi}|_{V}
and of

\overset{(\frac{n-3}{2})}{\psi}|_{{\mathrm{TT}}}
are elliptic, in the sense that they are restrictions to
V
, respectively to
{\mathrm{TT}}
of elliptic operators. Indeed, consider

\displaystyle{\cal L}_{{\mathrm{TT}}}(j)

\displaystyle:=-\frac{1}{(7-n+2j)}\left(\mathring{\Delta}_{T}+[4+j(6-n+j)]{{\varepsilon}}\right)\,,

(B.41)

\displaystyle\overset{(i)}{\psi}_{{\mathrm{TT}}}

\displaystyle:=\prod_{j=2}^{i}{\cal L}_{{\mathrm{TT}}}(\tfrac{n-5-2j}{2})\overset{(1)}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)\,,

(B.42)

where

\mathring{\Delta}_{T}h\equiv\left[\mathring{\Delta}+2(\mathring{\mathscr{R}}-(n-2){{\varepsilon}})\right]h\,.

(B.43)

The operators
\overset{(i)}{\psi}_{{\mathrm{TT}}}
are elliptic,
and the restriction
\overset{(\frac{n-3}{2})}{\psi}|_{{\mathrm{TT}}}
coincides with the restriction of
\overset{(i)}{\psi}_{{\mathrm{TT}}}
to
{\mathrm{TT}}
(compare (
4.21
), together with
(
J.23
) and (
J.32
), Appendix
J
below).

Similarly let

\displaystyle{\cal L}_{V}(j)

\displaystyle\equiv-\frac{(2+j)(4-n+j)}{(7-n+2j)(3-n+j)(3+j)}\left(\mathring{\Delta}_{T}+(1+j)(5-n+j){{\varepsilon}}\right)\,,

(B.44)

\displaystyle\overset{(\frac{n-3}{2})}{\psi}_{V}

\displaystyle:=\prod_{j=2}^{\frac{n-3}{2}}{\cal L}_{V}(\tfrac{n-5-2j}{2})\overset{(1)}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)\,;

(B.45)

cf. (
J.23
) and (
J.31
). The restriction of the elliptic operator
\overset{(\frac{n-3}{2})}{\psi}_{V}
to
V

coincides with
\overset{(\frac{n-3}{2})}{\psi}|_{V}
.

Ellipticity of
\overset{(\frac{n-3}{2})}{\psi}|_{V}
and of

\overset{(\frac{n-3}{2})}{\psi}|_{{\mathrm{TT}}}
, in the sense just explained,
shows that the image of
X^{k+n-3}
by
\overset{(\frac{n-3}{2})}{\psi}
is
L^{2}
-closed and equals
X^{k}
.
Lemma
B.11
gives

\displaystyle H^{k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big(S\cap H^{k}\big)\oplus\ker\big(\overset{(\frac{n-3}{2})}{\psi}|_{V\oplus{\mathrm{TT}}}\big)\oplus X^{k}

(B.46)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big(S\cap H^{k}\big)\oplus\ker\big(\overset{(\frac{n-3}{2})}{\psi}|_{V\oplus{\mathrm{TT}}}\big)\oplus\text{im}\big(\overset{(\frac{n-3}{2})}{\psi}|_{X^{k+n-3}}\big)\,,

as the first two summands are
L^{2}
-closed.

The proof for inconvenient pairs
(n,\ell)
with
i>\frac{n-3}{2}
is similar, based on
the
L^{2}
-orthogonal splittings

\displaystyle{\mathrm{TT}}\cap H^{k_{\gamma}+2i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ker\big(\overset{(i)}{\psi}|_{{\mathrm{TT}}}\big)\oplus\Big(\underbrace{\Big(\ker\big(\overset{(i)}{\psi}|_{{\mathrm{TT}}}\big)\Big)^{\perp}\cap H^{k_{\gamma}+2i}}_{=:X^{k+2i}}\Big)\,,

(B.47)

\displaystyle H^{k_{\gamma}+2i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underbrace{\big((S\oplus V)\cap H^{k_{\gamma}+2i}\big)\oplus\ker\big(\overset{(i)}{\psi}|_{{\mathrm{TT}}}\big)}_{=\ker\overset{(i)}{\psi}|_{H^{k_{\gamma}+2i}}}\oplus X^{k+2i}\,.

(B.48)

Indeed, ellipticity of
\overset{(i)}{\psi}|_{{\mathrm{TT}}}
shows that the image of
X^{k+2i}
by
\overset{(i)}{\psi}
is
L^{2}
-closed and equals
X^{k}
.
Lemma
B.11
applies as before.

B.7
\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(2)}}\circ\chi

Proposition
B.13
.

For
k\geq 0
the image

\text{im}_{k}\big(\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(2)}}\circ\overset{(i)}{\chi}\big):=\text{im}\big(\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(2)}}\circ\overset{(i)}{\chi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)|_{H^{k+2i+1}({{}^{d}M})}\big)

is closed in
H^{k}({{}^{d}M})
, and we have

\displaystyle\text{im}_{k}\big(\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(2)}}\circ\overset{(i)}{\chi}\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big(\ker\Big(\big(\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(2)}}\circ\overset{(i)}{\chi}\big)^{\dagger}\Big)\Big)^{\perp}\cap H^{k}({{}^{d}M})

(B.49)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\Big(\ker\big(\overset{(i)}{\chi}\circ\,C\big)\Big)^{\perp}\cap H^{k}({{}^{d}M})\,.

Proof.

Let us for conciseness write
\overset{(i)}{\chi}
for
\overset{(i)}{\chi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)
.
The commutation relation (
6.154
) shows that

\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(2)}}\circ\overset{(i)}{\chi}=\overset{(i)}{\tilde{\chi}}\circ\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(2)}}

(B.50)

with an elliptic operator
\overset{(i)}{\tilde{\chi}}
(cf. Proposition
C.9
below),
the precise form of which is irrelevant for the current purposes. This shows that
{\mathrm{TT}}\subset\ker(\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(2)}}\circ\overset{(i)}{\chi})
. The York splitting

H^{k+1}={\mathrm{TT}}\oplus\text{im}\,C

(B.51)

shows that

\text{im}_{k}\big(\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(2)}}\circ\overset{(i)}{\chi}\big)=\text{im}\,\big(\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(2)}}\circ\overset{(i)}{\chi}\circ\,C\big)=\text{im}\,\big(\overset{(i)}{\tilde{\chi}}\circ\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(2)}}\circ\,C\big)\,.

(B.52)

As
\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(2)}}\circ\,C
is elliptic, the operator at the right-hand side of the last equality is elliptic, and the result follows from
Proposition
B.1
.

Appendix C
Operators on
\mathbf{S}

The aim of this appendix is to analyse some further mapping properties of operators, acting on fields defined on cross-sections of
{\mycal N}
, as relevant for the problem at hand. The examples of main interest arise from null hypersurfaces in Birmingham-Kottler metrics, where the metric on cross-sections is Einstein. In this appendix we thus restrict ourselves to
compact
{d}
-dimensional Einstein manifolds

({}^{d}M,\mathring{\gamma})
,
{d}\geq 3
. The corresponding results with
{d}=2
can be found in
Czimek:2016ydb
;
ChCong1
.

Some of the arguments below are repetitive with these in
{d}=2
in
(
ChCong1
, Appendix C)
, we include them here for the convenience of the reader.

The results here hold mutatis mutandi in weighted Hölder spaces, where orthogonality is always understood in
L^{2}
.

C.1
The conformal Killing operator

Consider the
conformal Killing operator

C
on a closed
{d}
-dimensional Riemannian manifold
({}^{d}M,{\mathring{\gamma}})
:

\xi^{A}\mapsto\mathring{D}_{A}\xi_{B}+\mathring{D}_{B}\xi_{A}-\frac{2}{d}\mathring{D}^{C}\xi_{C}{\mathring{\gamma}}_{AB}=:2C(\xi)_{AB}\,,

(C.1)

where
\mathring{D}
is the covariant derivative operator of the metric
{\mathring{\gamma}}
.
When invoking
C(\xi)
we will always assume implicitly that
\xi\in H^{k}({{}^{d}M})
with some
k\geq 1
.

Recall that: we use the symbol
{\mathrm{CKV}}
to denote the space of conformal Killing vectors;
{\mathrm{TT}}
denotes the space of trace-free divergence-free symmetric two-tensors; orthogonality is defined in
L^{2}
;
\mathop{\mathrm{TS}}
denotes the trace-free symmetric part of a two-tensor.

We have:

Proposition
C.1
.

1.

There are no nontrivial Killing vectors or conformal Killing vectors on manifolds with negative Ricci tensor.

2.

On Ricci-flat manifolds all conformal Killing vectors are covariantly constant, hence Killing.

3.

\ker(\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(2)}}\circ\,C)={\mathrm{CKV}}
.

4.

\text{im}(\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(2)}}\circ\,C)={\mathrm{CKV}^{\perp}}
.

5.

For any vector field
\xi\in H^{1}({}^{d}M)
we have
C(\xi)^{[{\mathrm{TT}}]}=0
.

Proof.

1. and 2.:
Taking the divergence of the conformal Killing equation and commuting derivatives
leads to

\mathring{D}^{A}\mathring{D}_{A}\xi_{B}+\mathring{R}_{BC}\xi^{C}+\frac{{d}-2}{{d}}\mathring{D}_{B}\mathring{D}^{A}\xi_{A}=0\,.

(C.2)

Multiplying by
\xi^{B}
and integrating over
{}^{d}M
one finds

\int_{{}^{d}M}\big(|\mathring{D}\xi|^{2}+\frac{{d}-2}{{d}}|\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(1)}}\xi|^{2}-\mathring{R}_{BC}\xi^{B}\xi^{C}\big)\,d\mu_{{\mathring{\gamma}}}=0\,.

(C.3)

If
\mathring{R}_{BC}\leq 0
we find that
\xi
is covariantly constant, vanishing if
\mathring{R}_{BC}<0
.

3.
Let
\xi_{A}
be in the kernel of
\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(2)}}\circ\,C
, we have

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{{}^{d}M}\eta^{A}\mathring{D}_{B}(\mathring{D}_{A}\xi_{B}+\mathring{D}_{B}\xi_{A}-\frac{2}{d}\mathring{D}^{C}\xi_{C}{\mathring{\gamma}}_{AB})d\mu_{{\mathring{\gamma}}}=2\int_{{}^{d}M}\eta^{A}\mathring{D}^{B}\big(\mathop{\mathrm{TS}}(\mathring{D}_{A}\xi_{B})\big)d\mu_{{\mathring{\gamma}}}

(C.4)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\int_{{}^{d}M}\mathring{D}^{B}\eta^{A}\mathop{\mathrm{TS}}(\mathring{D}_{A}\xi_{B})d\mu_{{\mathring{\gamma}}}=-2\int_{{}^{d}M}\mathop{\mathrm{TS}}(\mathring{D}^{B}\eta^{A})\mathop{\mathrm{TS}}(\mathring{D}_{A}\xi_{B})d\mu_{{\mathring{\gamma}}}\,.

Setting
\xi=\eta
we conclude that
\eta
is a conformal Killing vector.

4.
The operator
L:=\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(2)}}\circ\,C
is elliptic and formally self-adjoint (since (
C.4
) is symmetric with respect to
the interchange of
\eta
and
\xi
), with

\ker L={\mathrm{CKV}}
, and
we conclude by Proposition
B.1
.

5. The field
C(\xi)^{[{\mathrm{TT}}]}
is obtained by
L^{2}
-projecting
C(\xi)
on
{\mathrm{TT}}
.
So let
h\in L^{2}
be a weak solution of the equation
\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(2)}}h=0
, i.e., for all
\xi\in H^{1}({}^{d}M)
we have

\int_{{}^{d}M}{\mathring{D}}^{A}\xi^{B}h_{AB}=0\,,

(C.5)

which is precisely the statement that the
L^{2}
-projection of
C(\xi)
on
{\mathrm{TT}}
vanishes.

Proposition
C.2
.

The conformal Killing operator on
{d}
-dimensional compact manifolds,
d\geq 3
, has

1.

a
{d}({d}+1)/2
dimensional kernel on
S^{d}
with the canonical metric;

2.

a
{d}
-dimensional kernel on flat manifolds;

3.

no kernel on negatively Ricci-curved
manifolds.

Proof.

1. The conformal group of a
{d}
-dimensional sphere is the same as the Lorentz group in
{d}+2
dimensions.

The statements about the kernel in points 2. and 3. follow from Proposition
C.1
.

\Box

We note the following properties of conformal Killing vectors on Einstein manifolds, which will be needed in what follows:

Lemma
C.3
.

Let
\chi^{A}\in{\mathrm{CKV}}
. If
({}^{d}M,{\mathring{\gamma}})
is Einstein,
then
{\mathring{D}}_{B}{\mathring{D}}_{A}\chi^{A}\in{\mathrm{CKV}}
.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{TS}}[{\mathring{D}}_{A}\chi_{B}]=0

\displaystyle\implies\frac{1}{d}{\mathring{\gamma}}_{AB}{\mathring{D}}^{C}\chi_{C}=\frac{1}{2}({\mathring{D}}_{A}\chi_{B}+{\mathring{D}}_{B}\chi_{A})

\displaystyle\implies-\frac{d-2}{d}{\mathring{D}}_{B}{\mathring{D}}^{C}\chi_{C}=\big(\mathring{\Delta}+(d-1){{\varepsilon}}\big)\chi_{B}\,,

(C.6)

and hence

-\frac{d-2}{d}\mathop{\mathrm{TS}}[{\mathring{D}}_{A}{\mathring{D}}_{B}{\mathring{D}}^{C}\chi_{C}]=\mathop{\mathrm{TS}}[{\mathring{D}}_{A}(\mathring{\Delta}+(d-1){{\varepsilon}})\chi_{B}]=\mathop{\mathrm{TS}}[{\mathring{D}}_{A}\mathring{\Delta}\chi_{B}]=0\,,

(C.7)

where the last equality follows from the commutation relation (
I.2
), p.
I.2
, using (
3.92
) for the vanishing of the
\mathring{\mathscr{R}}
-term there.

\Box

Lemma
C.4
.

Let
({}^{d}M,{\mathring{\gamma}})
be Einstein with scalar curvature equal to
d(d-1){{\varepsilon}}>0
. Then

(\mathring{\Delta}-(d-1){{\varepsilon}})\psi\in{\mathrm{CKV}}\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad\psi\in{\mathrm{CKV}}\,.

Proof.

For any vector field
\psi
we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(2)}}\circ\,C\circ(\mathring{\Delta}-(d-1){{\varepsilon}})\psi=(\mathring{\Delta}-(d-1){{\varepsilon}})\circ\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(2)}}\circ\,C(\psi)\,.

Keeping in mind that
{\mathrm{CKV}}
coincides with the kernel of
\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(2)}}\circ\,C
, the result follows from the fact that operators
\mathring{\Delta}-\lambda
with
\lambda>0
are isomorphisms.

\Box

C.2
Decompositions of two-tensors

A symmetric traceless
2
-covariant tensor field
h
on a compact
boundaryless
d
-dimensional Riemannian manifold
({}^{d}M,{\mathring{\gamma}})

can be uniquely split into a “scalar”, a “vector”, and a “tensor” part according to
(cf. e.g.
KodamaIshibashiMaster
)

h=h^{[S]}+h^{[V]}+h^{[TT]}\,,

(C.8)

where
h^{[TT]}
is a
{\mathrm{TT}}
-tensor,
h^{[V]}
is the Killing operator acting on a divergence-free vector, and
h^{[S]}
is the trace-free part of the Hessian of a function.

This can be compared to the following (unique) version of the York decomposition: for every symmetric two-covariant tensor

h\in H^{k}({}^{d}M)
,
k\geq 1
, we have

h_{AB}=\phi{\mathring{\gamma}}_{AB}+\frac{1}{2}\big({\mathring{D}}_{A}W_{B}+{\mathring{D}}_{B}W_{A}-\frac{2}{d}{\mathring{D}}^{k}W_{K}{\mathring{\gamma}}_{AB}\big)+h_{AB}^{[{\mathrm{TT}}]}\,,

(C.9)

where
\phi\in H^{k}({}^{d}M)
is obtained from the algebraic trace-part of
h
(and is zero when
h
is traceless, as almost always assumed elsewhere in this paper); then
h^{[{\mathrm{TT}}]}
is obtained by solving the conformal-vector-Laplacian equation for a unique vector field
W\in H^{k+1}({}^{d}M)\cap{\mathrm{CKV}^{\perp}}
.
Letting
\varphi\in H^{k+2}({}^{d}M)\cap\{1\}^{\perp}
be the unique solution of

\mathring{\Delta}\varphi={\mathring{D}}^{k}W_{K}\,,

(C.10)

we set

V_{A}=W_{A}-{\mathring{D}}_{A}\varphi\,.

(C.11)

This allows us to rewrite (
C.9
) as

h_{AB}=\big(\phi\hskip-22.76228pt\underbrace{-\frac{1}{d}\mathring{\Delta}\varphi\big){\mathring{\gamma}}_{AB}+{\mathring{D}}_{A}{\mathring{D}}_{B}\varphi}_{h^{[S]}\ \text{when $h$ is traceless, so that $\phi$ vanishes}}\hskip-22.76228pt+\underbrace{\frac{1}{2}\big({\mathring{D}}_{A}V_{B}+{\mathring{D}}_{B}V_{A}\big)}_{h^{[V]}}+\underbrace{h_{AB}^{[{\mathrm{TT}}]}}_{h^{[TT]}}\,,

(C.12)

which justifies (
C.8
).

C.3
Scalar-vector decomposition of vector fields

Let

\xi_{A}\in H^{k}
,
k\geq 1
. We define the vector subspaces
S,V\subset H^{k}
as

S=\{\xi_{A}\in H^{k}:\xi_{A}={\mathring{D}}_{A}\phi\,,\phi\in H^{k+1}({}^{d}M)\}\,,\quad V=\{\xi_{A}\in H^{k}:{\mathring{D}}^{A}\xi_{A}=0\}\,.

When
{}^{d}M
is compact and boundaryless the spaces
S
and
V
are
L^{2}
-orthogonal, and
any vector field
\xi
can be decomposed into its “scalar” and “vector” parts, denoted as

\displaystyle\xi=\xi^{[S]}+\xi^{[V]}\,,

(C.13)

with
\xi^{[S]}
and
\xi^{[V]}
given by

\mathring{\Delta}\phi={\mathring{D}}_{A}\xi^{A}\,,\quad\xi^{[S]}_{A}={\mathring{D}}_{A}\phi\,,\quad\xi^{[V]}_{A}=\xi_{A}-{\mathring{D}}_{A}\phi\,.

(C.14)

It will be useful for the following to note that on a Einstein manifold with scalar curvature equal to
(n-1)(n-2){{\varepsilon}}
, we have

\displaystyle{\mathring{D}}_{A}{\mathring{D}}^{B}\xi_{B}={\mathring{D}}_{A}{\mathring{D}}^{B}\xi^{[S]}_{B}={\mathring{D}}_{A}\mathring{\Delta}\phi=(\mathring{\Delta}-(n-2){{\varepsilon}}){\mathring{D}}_{A}\phi=(\mathring{\Delta}-(n-2){{\varepsilon}})\xi^{[S]}_{A}\,.

(C.15)

In the main text we use the following fact:

Proposition
C.5
.

Let
({}^{d}M,{\mathring{\gamma}})
be Einstein with scalar curvature equal to
(n-1)(n-2){{\varepsilon}}
. If
\xi\in{\mathrm{CKV}^{\perp}}\cap H^{k}({{}^{d}M})
,
k\geq 1
,
then

\xi_{A}^{[S]}\,,\ \xi_{A}^{[V]}\in{\mathrm{CKV}^{\perp}}\cap H^{k}({{}^{d}M})\,.

Proof:

Let
\xi\in{\mathrm{CKV}^{\perp}}
. By definition,
\xi^{[S]}_{A}
will be in
{\mathrm{CKV}^{\perp}}
if and only if for all conformal Killing
\chi^{A}
vectors we have

0=\int_{{}^{d}M}\chi^{A}\xi_{A}^{[S]}\equiv\int_{{}^{d}M}\chi^{A}{\mathring{D}}_{A}\phi\,d\mu_{\mathring{\gamma}}=-\int_{{}^{d}M}\phi{\mathring{D}}_{A}\chi^{A}\,d\mu_{\mathring{\gamma}}\,.

(C.16)

This will certainly be the case when either (see Proposition
C.1
): a)
{\mathrm{CKV}}=\{0\}
(which is the case on negatively Ricci-curved manifolds), or b)
{\mathrm{CKV}}={\mathrm{KV}}
(which is the case on Ricci-flat manifolds). So, to show that
\xi^{[S]}\in{\mathrm{CKV}^{\perp}}
it remains to consider positively curved manifolds.

Now, when
{{\varepsilon}}>0
the operator
(\mathring{\Delta}-(n-2){{\varepsilon}})
acting on tensors of any rank is an isomorphism. So, given a conformal Killing vector field
\chi
there exists vector field
\psi
such that

\chi=(\mathring{\Delta}-(n-2){{\varepsilon}})\psi\,.

By Lemma
C.4
we have
\psi\in{\mathrm{CKV}}
.
Then

\displaystyle\int_{{}^{d}M}\chi^{A}\xi_{A}^{[S]}\,d\mu_{\mathring{\gamma}}

\displaystyle=\int_{{}^{d}M}\big((\mathring{\Delta}-(n-2){{\varepsilon}})\psi^{A}\big)\xi_{A}^{[S]}\,d\mu_{\mathring{\gamma}}

(C.17)

\displaystyle=\int_{{}^{d}M}\psi^{A}\big(\mathring{\Delta}-(n-2){{\varepsilon}}\big)\xi_{A}^{[S]}\,d\mu_{\mathring{\gamma}}=\int_{{}^{d}M}\psi^{A}{\mathring{D}}_{A}{\mathring{D}}_{B}\xi^{B}\,d\mu_{\mathring{\gamma}}

\displaystyle=\int_{{}^{d}M}\underbrace{{\mathring{D}}_{B}{\mathring{D}}_{A}\psi^{A}}_{\in{\mathrm{CKV}}}\xi^{B}\,d\mu_{\mathring{\gamma}}=0\,,

(C.18)

where we used (
C.15
) and Lemma
C.3
.
Hence
\xi_{A}^{[S]}\in{\mathrm{CKV}^{\perp}}
in all cases.

Since
{\mathrm{CKV}^{\perp}}
is a vector space it holds that

\xi_{A}^{[V]}=\xi_{A}-\xi_{A}^{[S]}\in{\mathrm{CKV}^{\perp}}\,,

which finishes the proof.

C.4
{\chi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)

In this appendix we analyse the nature of the operators
\overset{(i)}{\chi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)
appearing in (
4.3
).
We work on an
(n-1)
-dimensional closed manifold,
n\geq 4
.
We begin with the following lemma:

Lemma
C.6
.

Let
a,c\in\mathbb{R}
. The operator

\operatorname{L}_{a,c}=aP+\mathring{\Delta}+2\mathring{\mathscr{R}}+c

acting on symmetric,
{\mathring{\gamma}}
-trace-free
2
-tensors is
formally self-adjoint, and elliptic if

a\neq-2,\,\frac{1-n}{n-2}\,.

Proof.

We start by noting that the operators
P
,
\mathring{\Delta}
and
\mathring{\mathscr{R}}
are all formally self-adjoint,
and thus so is
\operatorname{L}_{a,c}
.

Next,
let
k\neq 0
and let
\sigma(k)_{AB}
be the symbol of the principal part of
\operatorname{L}_{a,c}
, with kernel determined by the equation

\sigma(k)_{AB}\equiv k^{C}k_{C}h_{AB}+\frac{a}{2}(k_{A}k^{C}h_{CB}+k_{B}k^{C}h_{CA}-\frac{2}{n-1}k^{C}k^{D}h_{CD}{\mathring{\gamma}}_{AB})=0\,.

(C.19)

Contracting with
k^{A}k^{B}
gives

\displaystyle 0=k^{A}k^{B}\sigma(k)_{AB}

\displaystyle=(|k|^{2}+2\frac{a}{2}|k|^{2}-\frac{a}{n-1}|k|^{2})k^{A}k^{B}h_{AB}

\displaystyle=\left(1+a\left(1-\frac{1}{n-1}\right)\right)|k|^{2}k^{A}k^{B}h_{AB}\,

(C.20)

so that

\text{either (a) }k^{A}k^{B}h_{AB}=0\,,\ \text{or (b) }a=\frac{1-n}{n-2}\,.

(C.21)

Substituting case (a) into (
C.19
) gives

|k|^{2}h_{AB}+\frac{a}{2}(k_{A}k^{C}h_{CB}+k_{B}k^{C}h_{CA})=0\,.

(C.22)

Contracting with
k^{B}
gives

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=|k|^{2}k^{B}h_{AB}+\frac{a}{2}(k_{A}\underbrace{k^{B}k^{C}h_{CB}}_{=0}+|k|^{2}k^{C}h_{CA})

(C.23)

\displaystyle=\bigg(\frac{a}{2}+1\bigg)|k|^{2}k^{C}h_{CA}\,,

(C.24)

and hence

\text{either ($\alpha$) }a=-2\,,\ \text{or ($\beta$) }k^{C}h_{CA}=0\,.

(C.25)

Finally, substituting
(\beta)
into (
C.22
) gives

|k|^{2}h_{AB}=0

(C.26)

which completes the proof.

\Box

Using the last lemma one easily concludes that the operators

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}\left(k,\mathring{\Delta},P\right)

\displaystyle:=-\frac{1}{7-n+2k}\bigg[\frac{2(n-1)P}{(3+k)(3-n+k)}+2\mathring{\mathscr{R}}+\mathring{\Delta}-(n-4-k)(2+k){{\varepsilon}}\bigg]

(C.27)

if
\frac{\mathbbm{Z}}{2}\ni k\not\in\{-3,n-3,(n-7)/2\}
, and

\mathring{\mathcal{K}}\left(-3,\mathring{\Delta},P\right)=\frac{1}{n-1}\bigg[\frac{(n(1-n)-2)P}{n}+2\mathring{\mathscr{R}}+\mathring{\Delta}+(n-1){{\varepsilon}}\bigg]\,,

(C.28)

are elliptic. The remaining cases of interest, namely
\mathcal{K}\left(-1,\mathring{\Delta},P\right)
and
\mathcal{K}\left(-2,\mathring{\Delta},P\right)
, will be dealt with in the next section.

To continue, we have from (
4.6
):

\displaystyle\overset{(i)}{\chi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)

\displaystyle=\prod_{j=1}^{i}\mathcal{K}\left(-(j+3),\mathring{\Delta},P\right)\,.

(C.29)

Since the composition of elliptic operators is elliptic, we have shown:

Proposition
C.7
.

The operators
\overset{(i)}{\chi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)
are
formally self-adjoint and elliptic.

The proof of the following Lemma is similar to that of Lemma
C.8
, we leave the details to the reader.

Lemma
C.8
.

Let
a,c\in\mathbb{R}
. The operator

\tilde{L}_{a,c}=a\,\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(2)}}\circ\,C+\mathring{\Delta}+c

is
formally self-adjoint, and elliptic if

a\neq-2,\,\frac{1-n}{n-2}\,.

\Box

Recall (cf. below (
6.155
), p.
6.155
) that the operators

\overset{(p)}{\tilde{\chi}}(\mathring{\Delta},\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(2)}}\circ\,C)

are defined by replacing all appearances of the operators
P
and
2\mathring{\mathscr{R}}
in
\overset{(p)}{\chi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)
by
\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(2)}}\circ\,C
and
(n-2){{\varepsilon}}
respectively.
It then readily follows
from Lemma
C.8
that:

Proposition
C.9
.

The operators
\overset{(p)}{\tilde{\chi}}(\mathring{\Delta},\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(2)}}\circ\,C)
are
formally self-adjoint and elliptic.

C.5
\psi(\mathring{\Delta},P)

Let us now consider the operators involved in gluing the fields
\overset{(i)}{q}_{AB}
. Recall that the relevant operators are

\displaystyle\overset{(i)}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)

\displaystyle=\prod_{j=2}^{i}\mathcal{K}\left(\tfrac{n-5-2j}{2},\mathring{\Delta},P\right)\overset{(1)}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)\,\,,

(C.30)

\displaystyle\overset{(1)}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)

\displaystyle=-\bigg[\frac{4}{n+1}P-\mathring{\mathscr{R}}-\frac{1}{2}\mathring{\Delta}+\frac{(n-3)(n-1){{\varepsilon}}}{8}\bigg]\,,

(C.31)

for all
i\geq 1
.

By Lemma
C.6
the operator
\overset{(1)}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)

is elliptic for
n\neq 3,5
. However, we focus on
n>3
in this work; the case
n=5
will be addressed below.

Now, a straightforward calculation shows that the operators
\mathcal{K}\left(-1,\mathring{\Delta},P\right)
and
\mathcal{K}\left(-2,\mathring{\Delta},P\right)
fail to be elliptic only for odd
n>3
. All other
\mathcal{K}\left(k,\mathring{\Delta},P\right)
operators appearing in the
\overset{(i)}{\psi}
’s are elliptic.

This implies in particular, that:

Proposition
C.10
.

For convenient pairs
(n,k)
the operators
\overset{(i)}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)
are elliptic and formally self-adjoint.

Let us consider now an inconvenient pair
(n,k)
, where we must be careful about which fields are involved in the gluing.
For this we make use of the York decomposition for
h_{AB}
(cf. (
C.9
)).

First, the restriction of
\operatorname{L}_{a,c}
acting
{\mathrm{TT}}
is elliptic, since
Ph^{[{\mathrm{TT}}]}=0
and
\mathring{\Delta}
is elliptic.

Next, the non-elliptic
\mathcal{K}
operators can be seen to appear (when
n
is odd) in all the operators

\overset{(\frac{n-1}{2}+j)}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)=\overset{(j-1)}{\chi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)\mathring{\mathcal{K}}\left(-3,\mathring{\Delta},P\right)\underbrace{\mathcal{K}\left(-2,\mathring{\Delta},P\right)\mathcal{K}\left(-1,\mathring{\Delta},P\right)\overset{(\frac{n-5}{2})}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)}_{\equiv\overset{(\frac{n-1}{2})}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)}

(C.32)

with
j\geq 1
. This expression follows from the recursion relations (
A.33
)-(
A.34
).

We will use the underbraced term in (
C.32
) to show the following:

Proposition
C.11
.

We have, for
n\geq 5
odd and
j\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\overset{(\frac{n-3}{2}+j)}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)\,h^{[S]}\equiv 0\,,\quad\overset{(\frac{n-1}{2}+j)}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)\,h^{[V]}\equiv 0\,,

(C.33)

\displaystyle\overset{(\frac{n-1}{2}+j)}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)\circ\,C(W)\equiv 0\,,

(C.34)

\displaystyle\overset{(\frac{n-1}{2}+j)}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)\circ P\,(h)\equiv 0\,.

(C.35)

Proof.

The starting point is to consider the operator
P
acting on
{\mathrm{TT}^{\perp}}
-tensors. That is,

\displaystyle PC(W)_{AB}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mathop{\mathrm{TS}}[{\mathring{D}}_{A}{\mathring{D}}^{D}({\mathring{D}}_{D}W_{B}+{\mathring{D}}_{B}W_{D}-\frac{2}{n-1}{\mathring{\gamma}}_{BD}{\mathring{D}}^{C}W_{C})]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mathop{\mathrm{TS}}[{\mathring{D}}_{A}\mathring{\Delta}W_{B}+{\mathring{D}}_{A}{\mathring{D}}^{D}{\mathring{D}}_{B}W_{D}-\frac{2}{n-1}{\mathring{D}}_{A}{\mathring{D}}_{B}{\mathring{D}}^{C}W_{C}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mathop{\mathrm{TS}}[{\mathring{D}}_{A}\mathring{\Delta}W_{B}+\frac{n-3}{n-1}{\mathring{D}}_{A}{\mathring{D}}_{B}{\mathring{D}}^{C}W_{C}-{\mathring{D}}_{A}\mathring{R}^{E}{}_{D}{}^{D}{}_{B}W_{E}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mathop{\mathrm{TS}}[\mathring{\Delta}{\mathring{D}}_{A}W_{B}-(n-2){{\varepsilon}}{\mathring{D}}_{A}W_{B}-2\mathring{R}^{D}{}_{B}{}_{A}{}^{C}{\mathring{D}}_{C}W_{D}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+\frac{n-3}{n-1}{\mathring{D}}_{A}{\mathring{D}}_{B}{\mathring{D}}^{C}W_{C}+(n-2){{\varepsilon}}{\mathring{D}}_{A}W_{B}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mathop{\mathrm{TS}}\big[\mathring{\Delta}{\mathring{D}}_{A}W_{B}]+\mathring{\mathscr{R}}({\mathring{D}}W)_{AB}+\frac{n-3}{2(n-1)}\mathop{\mathrm{TS}}[{\mathring{D}}_{A}{\mathring{D}}_{B}{\mathring{D}}^{C}W_{C}\big]\,;

(C.36)

recall that
\mathring{\mathscr{R}}
has been defined in (
3.91
).
Thus

PC(W)_{AB}=\left(\frac{1}{2}\mathring{\Delta}+\mathring{\mathscr{R}}\right)C(W)_{AB}+\frac{n-3}{2(n-1)}\mathop{\mathrm{TS}}[{\mathring{D}}_{A}{\mathring{D}}_{B}{\mathring{D}}^{C}W_{C}]\,.

(C.37)

Next we make use of the scalar-vector-tensor decomposition (
C.12
) to write this as

\displaystyle PC(W)_{AB}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle P(h^{[S]}+h^{[V]})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{2}\mathring{\Delta}+\mathring{\mathscr{R}}\right)\left(\mathop{\mathrm{TS}}[{\mathring{D}}_{A}{\mathring{D}}_{B}\varphi]+{\mathring{D}}_{(A}V_{B)}\right)+\frac{n-3}{2(n-1)}\mathop{\mathrm{TS}}[{\mathring{D}}_{A}{\mathring{D}}_{B}\mathring{\Delta}\varphi]\,,

where we have also used (
C.10
). This means that we can immediately write for the vector part

P(h^{[V]})_{AB}=\frac{1}{2}(\mathring{\Delta}+2\mathring{\mathscr{R}})h^{[V]}_{AB}\,.

(C.39)

Second, focusing on the scalar part. We commute the derivatives (using (
I.1
) in the second line, (
I.2
) in the third line, and (
3.92
) in the fourth one)

\displaystyle P(h^{[S]})

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{2}\mathring{\Delta}+\mathring{\mathscr{R}}\right)\mathop{\mathrm{TS}}[{\mathring{D}}_{A}{\mathring{D}}_{B}\varphi]+\frac{n-3}{2(n-1)}\mathop{\mathrm{TS}}[{\mathring{D}}_{A}{\mathring{D}}_{B}\mathring{\Delta}\varphi]

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{2}\mathring{\Delta}+\mathring{\mathscr{R}}\right)h^{[S]}+\frac{n-3}{2(n-1)}\mathop{\mathrm{TS}}[{\mathring{D}}_{A}(\mathring{\Delta}-(n-2){{\varepsilon}}){\mathring{D}}_{B}\varphi]

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{2}\mathring{\Delta}+\mathring{\mathscr{R}}\right)h^{[S]}+\frac{n-3}{2(n-1)}\mathop{\mathrm{TS}}[(\mathring{\Delta}+2[\mathring{\mathscr{R}}-(n-2)\epsilon]){\mathring{D}}_{A}{\mathring{D}}_{B}\varphi]

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{2}\mathring{\Delta}+\mathring{\mathscr{R}}\right)h^{[S]}+\frac{n-3}{2(n-1)}(\mathring{\Delta}+2[\mathring{\mathscr{R}}-(n-2)\epsilon])h^{[S]}

\displaystyle=\frac{n-2}{n-1}\left(\mathring{\Delta}+2\mathring{\mathscr{R}}-(n-3){{\varepsilon}}\right)h^{[S]}_{AB}\,.

(C.40)

Putting these results together we have

\displaystyle P(h^{[S]})_{AB}

\displaystyle=\frac{n-2}{n-1}\left(\mathring{\Delta}+2\mathring{\mathscr{R}}-(n-3){{\varepsilon}}\right)h^{[S]}_{AB}\,,

(C.41)

\displaystyle P(h^{[V]})_{AB}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(\mathring{\Delta}+2\mathring{\mathscr{R}})h^{[V]}_{AB}\,.

(C.42)

We are ready now to prove the first part of (
C.33
):
for
j\geq 0

\overset{(\frac{n-3}{2}+j)}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)\,h^{[S]}=0\,.

(C.43)

This follows from substituting (
C.41
) into the definition of
\overset{(\frac{n-3}{2})}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)\,h^{[S]}
. To see this in the case
n=5
, using (
A.31
) we find

\displaystyle\overset{(\frac{n-3}{2})}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)\,h^{[S]}

\displaystyle=\overset{(1)}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)\,h^{[S]}=\frac{1}{2}\left(-\frac{4}{3}P+(\mathring{\Delta}+2\mathring{\mathscr{R}})-2{{\varepsilon}}\right)h^{[S]}\,

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(-\frac{4}{3}\times\frac{5-2}{5-1}(\mathring{\Delta}+2\mathring{\mathscr{R}}-(5-3){{\varepsilon}})+(\mathring{\Delta}+2\mathring{\mathscr{R}})-2{{\varepsilon}}\right)h^{[S]}

\displaystyle=0\,.

(C.44)

Next for
n>5
,
from (
A.33
) we have

\overset{(\frac{n-3}{2})}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)=\overset{(\frac{n-5}{2})}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)\,\mathcal{K}\left(-1,\mathring{\Delta},P\right)\,,

(C.45)

and from (
A.6
)

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}\left(-1,\mathring{\Delta},P\right)h^{[S]}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n-5}\left(-\frac{n-1}{n-2}P+\mathring{\Delta}+2\mathring{\mathscr{R}}-(n-3)\epsilon\right)h^{[S]}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n-5}\Bigg[-\left(\frac{n-1}{n-2}\right)\left(\frac{n-2}{n-1}\big(\mathring{\Delta}+2\mathring{\mathscr{R}}-(n-3){{\varepsilon}}\big)\right)

\displaystyle+\mathring{\Delta}+2\mathring{\mathscr{R}}-(n-3)\epsilon\Bigg]h^{[S]}=0\,.

(C.46)

A similar calculation using (
C.42
) shows that this does
not
hold when acting on
h^{[V]}
. Instead we have

\mathcal{K}\left(-1,\mathring{\Delta},P\right)h^{[V]}=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{6}\left(\mathring{\Delta}+2(\mathring{\mathscr{R}}-3\epsilon)\right)h^{[V]}\quad\text{if }n=5\,,\\
\frac{(n-3)}{2(n-2)(n-5)}\left(\mathring{\Delta}+2(\mathring{\mathscr{R}}-(n-2)\epsilon)\right)h^{[V]}\quad\text{if }n>5\,.\end{cases}

(C.47)

It follows that the principal symbol of the restriction of
\mathcal{K}\left(-1,\mathring{\Delta},P\right)
on
\{h:\,h=h^{[V]}\}
comes only from
\mathring{\Delta}
and is thus elliptic.

We move on now to the operator

\displaystyle\overset{(\frac{n-1}{2})}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)=\overset{(\frac{n-3}{2})}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)\,\mathcal{K}\left(-2,\mathring{\Delta},P\right)\,.

(C.48)

Using the definitions of the operators (see (
A.6
)) and substituting for
Ph^{[V]}
with (
C.42
), we have

\mathcal{K}\left(-2,\mathring{\Delta},P\right)h^{[V]}\propto\left(P-\frac{1}{2}(\mathring{\Delta}+2\mathring{\mathscr{R}})\right)h^{[V]}=0\,,

(C.49)

and so we obtain the second part of (
C.33
): for
j\geq 0
,

\overset{(\frac{n-1}{2}+j)}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)\,h^{[V]}=0\,.

(C.50)

Now, writing
C(W)
as
h^{[S]}+h^{[V]}
, (
C.34
) follows from (
C.33
):

\overset{(\frac{n-1}{2}+j)}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)\big(C(W)\big)=0\,.

(C.51)

Finally, to obtain (
C.35
)
we write

\displaystyle\overset{(\frac{n-1}{2}+j)}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)\circ P\,(h)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\overset{(\frac{n-1}{2}+j)}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)\circ P\,\big(h^{[{\mathrm{TT}}]}+C(W)\big)

(C.52)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\overset{(\frac{n-1}{2}+j)}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)\circ P\,\big(C(W)\big)=P\circ\overset{(\frac{n-1}{2}+j)}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)\,\big(C(W)\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

by (
C.51
),
since
\overset{(i)}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)
commutes with
P

(see (
I.8
), p.
I.8
below)
and
P(h^{[{\mathrm{TT}}]})=0
.

\Box

Summarising, we have the following:

Proposition
C.12
.

For inconvenient pairs
(n,k)
the operators
\overset{(i)}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)

1.

acting on
h^{[S]}
are elliptic for
i<\frac{n-3}{2}
and vanish for
i\geq\frac{n-3}{2}
;

2.

acting on
h^{[V]}
:

(a)

are elliptic for
i\leq\frac{n-3}{2}
,

(b)

vanish when
i\geq\frac{n-1}{2}
;

3.

acting on
h^{[{\mathrm{TT}}]}
are elliptic.

We end this section with a remark on the kernel of the operator
\mathcal{K}\left(-1,\mathring{\Delta},P\right)
. First,
\mathcal{K}\left(-1,\mathring{\Delta},P\right)
vanishes identically on
S
:
\mathcal{K}\left(-1,\mathring{\Delta},P\right)h^{[S]}=0
.
Next, from (
C.47
) and the commutation relation (
I.2
) we have,
for vector fields with vanishing divergence,

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}\left(-1,\mathring{\Delta},P\right)h^{[V]}\propto

\displaystyle\left(\mathring{\Delta}+2(\mathring{\mathscr{R}}-(n-2)\epsilon)\right){\mathring{D}}_{(A}V_{B)}={\mathring{D}}_{(A}\left(\mathring{\Delta}-(n-2)\epsilon\right)V_{B)}\,.

(C.53)

Now, the operator inside of
{\mathring{D}}_{A}
on the right-hand side of the last equality is just the (negative of) the Hodge Laplacian.
Therefore, any
h^{[V]}_{AB}={\mathring{D}}_{(A}V_{B)}
constructed from a harmonic vector lies in the kernel of
\mathcal{K}\left(-1,\mathring{\Delta},P\right)
. Moreover, expanding any
V_{A}
, which generates an
h^{[V]}_{AB}
lying in the kernel of
\mathcal{K}\left(-1,\mathring{\Delta},P\right)
, in an orthonormal eigenbasis of the Hodge Laplacian implies either
V
is a Killing vector (in which case
h^{[V]}_{AB}=0
) or
V
is harmonic. Together, this implies

\ker\left(\mathcal{K}\left(-1,\mathring{\Delta},P\right)\big|_{h^{[V]}}\right)=\left\{h^{[V]}={\mathring{D}}_{(A}V_{B)}\,\,:\,\,{\mathring{D}}_{[A}V_{B]}=0={\mathring{D}}^{A}V_{A}\right\}\,.

(C.54)

C.6
The gauge operators
\operatorname{\!\!\check{\,\,L}}_{n}
and
\operatorname{L_{n}}

In this section we analyse the gauge operators involved in the gauge transformations of the radial charges associated to
\overset{(\frac{n-3}{2}+j)}{q}_{AB}
,
j\geq 0
, with
n
odd. In particular, we wish to show that the gauge-invariant charges in the
m=0=\alpha
case (cf. Table
1.3
) associated to these fields are smooth and live in a finite dimensional space.

We will make use of the following commutation relation:

\displaystyle{\mathring{D}}^{A}\big(a\,\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(2)}}\circ\,C+\mathring{\Delta}\big)\xi_{A}=\Big((\frac{a(n-2)}{n-1}+1)\mathring{\Delta}+(a+1)(n-2){{\varepsilon}}\Big){\mathring{D}}^{A}\xi_{A}\,.

(C.55)

We begin the analysis with
j=0
, with the relevant operator being
\operatorname{\!\!\check{\,\,L}}_{n}
of
(
4.43
).
We wish to show:

Proposition
C.13
.

\ker(\operatorname{\!\!\check{\,\,L}}_{n}^{\dagger})\cap\ker(\overset{(\frac{n-3}{2})}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P))
is smooth and finite dimensional.

Proof.

From the expression (
4.43
) of
\operatorname{\!\!\check{\,\,L}}_{n}
, the principal symbol of
\operatorname{\!\!\check{\,\,L}}_{n}
arises from the operator

c_{n}\overset{((n-5)/2)}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)\,P\circ\,C\circ{\mathring{D}}

acting on functions,
with a constant
c_{n}
depending upon the dimension
n
.

We first analyse the nature of the operator

\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(1)}}\circ\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(2)}}\circ\overset{((n-5)/2)}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)\circ P\circ\,C\circ{\mathring{D}}\,.

(C.56)

For this let us consider the kernel of the symbol of the self-adjoint operator
\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(1)}}\circ\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(2)}}\circ P\circ\,C\circ{\mathring{D}}
: for
k\neq 0
, we have

\displaystyle 0=\sigma(k)(\xi^{u})

\displaystyle:=k^{A}k^{B}\mathop{\mathrm{TS}}\big[k_{A}k^{C}\mathop{\mathrm{TS}}[k_{C}k_{B}\xi^{u}]\big]

\displaystyle=\frac{(n-2)^{2}}{(n-1)^{2}}|k|^{6}\xi^{u}\iff\xi^{u}=0\,.

(C.57)

Thus, for
n\neq 1,2
, the operator

\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(1)}}\circ\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(2)}}\circ P\circ\,C\circ{\mathring{D}}

is elliptic.

Next, we write
