Figure G.2:
The submatrix
G^{[X]}
for
k>(n-3)/2
.

The matrix for
X=V
can be read from Figure
G.2
after deleting the
second row and second column there; the matrix for
k=\frac{n-3}{2}
is obtained after deleting the first row and first column there.
The new boxed entries in Figure
G.2
, as compared to (
6.4.3
), are

\displaystyle\overset{(*)}{\tilde{\chi}}_{4-n}

\displaystyle:=1\,,\quad\overset{(*)}{\tilde{\psi}}_{5-n}:=(\mathring{\Delta}-(n-2){{\varepsilon}})|_{\mathring{\Delta}\mapsto\lambda_{\ell}}\,,\quad\overset{(0)}{\tilde{\chi}}_{4}=1\,,

(G.20)

where the second term is only relevant for
X=S
.
Now, we have

(\mathring{\Delta}-(n-2){{\varepsilon}})|_{\mathring{\Delta}\mapsto\lambda_{\ell}}\neq 0\,,

equivalently,
(\mathring{\Delta}-(n-2){{\varepsilon}})\eta_{\ell}^{[S]}\neq 0
, as follows from the commutation relation (cf. (
I.1
))

\displaystyle(\mathring{\Delta}-(n-2){{\varepsilon}}){\mathring{D}}_{A}\phi={\mathring{D}}_{A}\mathring{\Delta}\phi\,,

(G.21)

therefore, if we write
\eta_{\ell}^{[S]}={\mathring{D}}_{A}\phi_{\ell}
, then

\displaystyle(\mathring{\Delta}-(n-2){{\varepsilon}})\eta_{\ell}^{[S]}=0\ \iff\ \mathring{\Delta}\phi_{\ell}=0\ \iff\ \phi_{\ell}=\mbox{\rm const}\ \iff\ \eta_{\ell}^{[S]}=0\,.

(G.22)

It readily follows that
\overset{(*)}{\tilde{\psi}}_{5-n}
does not vanish for such modes.
Since all the remaining boxed entries of
G^{[X]}
are
\ell
-independent non-zero numbers, we conclude that
G^{[X]}\circ\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(2)}}\circ\,C
has trivial kernel on modes with
\eta_{\ell}\in{\mathrm{CKV}^{\perp}}
. Furthermore, the regularity of the solution is as given in (
F.22
) and (
F.23
) with
p\in(\{n-4,n-5\}\cup[\frac{9-n}{2},1]\cup[4,4+k])\cap\mathbbm{Z}
. The proof of this follows analogously to that of Corollary
F.2
and is left to the readers.

Finally, let us consider the
{\mathrm{TT}}
projection of the fields
\overset{(p)}{r}_{AB}
,
1\leq p\leq k
. The transport equations for these fields are coupled amongst themselves, but are not coupled with any other fields. As a result, the analysis is very similar to that in the proof of Theorem
6.6
. Indeed, the equations we are looking at is the
{\mathrm{TT}}
projection of (
6.109
):

\displaystyle\underbrace{(\overset{(p)}{q}_{AB}\big|_{\mathbf{S}_{2}}-\overset{(p)}{q}_{AB}\big|_{\mathbf{S}_{1}}+\text{known fields})^{[{\mathrm{TT}}]}}_{=:\overset{(p)}{r}{}^{[{\mathrm{TT}}]}_{AB}}

\displaystyle=\overset{(p)}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)\,\overset{[(7-n+2p)/2]}{{\hat{\varphi}}_{AB}}{}^{[{\mathrm{TT}}]}+\alpha^{2p}\overset{(p)}{\psi}_{[\alpha]}\overset{[(7-n-2p)/2]}{{\hat{\varphi}}_{AB}}{}{}^{[{\mathrm{TT}}]}+m^{p}\overset{(p)}{\psi}_{[m]}\overset{[(7-n+2p(n-1))/2]}{{\hat{\varphi}}_{AB}}{}{}^{[{\mathrm{TT}}]}

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{j,\ell}^{p_{**}}m^{j}\alpha^{2\ell}\overset{(p)}{\psi}_{j,\ell}(\mathring{\Delta},P)\,\overset{[p-\frac{n-7}{2}+j(n-2)-2\ell]}{{\hat{\varphi}}_{AB}}{}{}^{[{\mathrm{TT}}]}\,,\quad 1\leq p\leq k\,;

(G.23)

recall that
\overset{(p)}{\psi}_{[\alpha]}=0
for
p>\frac{n-1}{2}
, and that “known fields” refer to the contribution from the field
v
. We then have the following:

Theorem
G.1
.

Let the pair
(n,k)
be inconvenient.
The system (
G.23
) can be solved by a choice of interpolating fields

\overset{[j]}{\hat{\varphi}}{}{}^{[{\mathrm{TT}}]}_{AB}\in H^{k_{\gamma}}(\mathbf{S})\,,\quad j\in\left[\max\{\frac{7-n-2k}{2},4-n\},\frac{7-n+2k(n-1)}{2}\right]

for any finite
k
. Its solutions are determined by an elliptic system, uniquely up to possibly a finite number of eigenfunctions of the Laplacian acting on tensors.

The proof of this theorem proceeds in a way identical to that of Theorem
6.6
with the following minor modifications:

1.

All two-covariant tensor fields appearing there are replaced with their
{\mathrm{TT}}
projections.

2.

The coefficients
\overset{(p)}{\tilde{\psi}}_{\frac{7-n-2p}{2}}
are still equal to
\alpha^{2p}\overset{(p)}{\psi}_{[\alpha]}
(cf. (
G.4
)), and
are non-zero for
p\leq\frac{n+1}{2}
. Now they vanish for
p>\frac{n+1}{2}
, so that the matrix
A_{k}
is no longer surjective. This does not affect the solvability of the system, but only removes the option of setting
\Omega_{k}=0=\Xi_{k}
.

3.

Finally, regularity follows from ellipticity of
\overset{(p)}{\psi}(\mathring{\Delta},P)
acting on
{\mathrm{TT}}
.

Appendix H
Linearized Kerr-deSitter in Bondi Gauge

The full Kerr-(A)dS metric in
(n+1)
-spacetime dimensions is given by

Gibbons:2004js
:
2
2

2

To facilitate the comparison with that reference we note that
a_{i}
is the negative of the one there, and recall that
a_{N+\delta}=0
for
\delta=1
.

\displaystyle ds^{2}=

\displaystyle-W(1-\alpha^{2}r^{2})dt^{2}+\frac{2m}{U}\Bigl(Wdt+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{a_{i}\mu_{i}^{2}d\phi_{i}}{\Xi_{i}}\Bigr)^{2}

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{r^{2}+a_{i}^{2}}{\Xi_{i}}\bigl(\mu_{i}^{2}d\phi_{i}^{2}+d\mu_{i}^{2})+\frac{Udr^{2}}{V-2m}+\delta r^{2}d\mu_{N+\delta}^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{\alpha^{2}}{W(1-\alpha^{2}r^{2})}\Bigl(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{r^{2}+a_{i}^{2}}{\Xi_{i}}\mu_{i}d\mu_{i}+\delta r^{2}\mu_{N+\delta}d\mu_{N+\delta}\Bigr)^{2}\,,

(H.1)

where

\displaystyle W

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\mu_{i}^{2}}{\Xi_{i}}+\delta\mu_{N+\delta}^{2}\,,\quad V=r^{\delta-2}(1-\alpha^{2}r^{2})\prod_{i=1}^{N}(r^{2}+a_{i}^{2})\,,

\displaystyle U

\displaystyle=\frac{V}{1-\alpha^{2}r^{2}}\Bigl(1-\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{a_{i}^{2}\mu_{i}^{2}}{r^{2}+a_{i}^{2}}\Bigr)\,,\quad\Xi_{i}=1+\alpha^{2}a_{i}^{2}\,.

(H.2)

Here,
\delta=1,0
for even, odd dimensions,
N=\bigl[\frac{n}{2}\bigr]
(where
[A]
denotes the integer part of
A
), and the coordinates
\mu_{i}
obey a constraint

\sum_{i=1}^{N+\delta}\mu_{i}^{2}=1\,.

(H.3)

In these coordinates there is no rotation at spatial infinity and the angles
\phi_{i}
are periodic with period
2\pi
.

We linearise the metric by expanding to linear order in the various rotation parameters
a_{i}
which gives the higher dimensional Lense–Thirring metric

\displaystyle ds^{2}_{LT}

\displaystyle=-fdt^{2}+\frac{dr^{2}}{f}+r^{2}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mu_{i}^{2}d\phi_{i}^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{N+\delta}\!d\mu_{i}^{2}\right)+\frac{4m}{r^{n-2}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}a_{i}\mu_{i}^{2}dtd\phi_{i}+O(a_{i}^{2})\,,

(H.4)

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle=\!1-\alpha^{2}r^{2}-\frac{2m}{r^{n-2}}+O(a_{i}^{2})\,.

(H.5)

We can rewrite this as a perturbation on the Birmingham--Kottler metrics
3
3

3

At least for spherical topologies.

(
3.5
) by noting that the term in the brackets of (
H.4
) is just the metric on the
(n-1)
-sphere
{\mathring{\gamma}}_{AB}dx^{A}dx^{B}
and by performing the coordinate transformation

dt=du+\frac{dr}{f}\,.

(H.6)

Then we have,

ds^{2}_{LT}={\mathring{g}}_{\alpha\beta}dx^{\alpha}dx^{\beta}+\frac{4m}{r^{n-2}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}a_{i}\mu_{i}^{2}d\phi_{i}(du+dr/f)+O(a_{i}^{2})\,.

(H.7)

This is manifestly not in Bondi gauge as there are
g_{rA}
components, however we can address this in two ways which turn out to be equivalent. First, outside of all horizons, we could accompany the change from the
t
- to the
u
-coordinate with
r
-dependent rotations of the
(\mu_{i},\phi_{i})
planes,

d\varphi_{i}=d\phi_{i}-\frac{2ma_{i}}{r^{n}f}dr\,,

(H.8)

which transforms the metric to

ds^{2}_{LT}={\mathring{g}}_{\alpha\beta}dx^{\alpha}dx^{\beta}+\frac{4m}{r^{n-2}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}a_{i}\mu_{i}^{2}d\varphi_{i}du+O(a_{i}^{2})\,.

(H.9)

Second we could perform an infinitesimal gauge transformation generated by
\zeta^{\mu}
to put the perturbation into Bondi form (à la Section 3 of
ChHMS
). Denoting the perturbation in (
H.7
) by
h_{\mu\nu}
, the vector
\zeta
satisfies

\displaystyle{\cal L}_{\zeta}(g_{rr}+h_{rr})

\displaystyle=0\,,

(H.10)

\displaystyle{\cal L}_{\zeta}(g_{rA}+h_{rA})

\displaystyle=0\,,

(H.11)

\displaystyle g^{AB}{\cal L}_{\zeta}(g_{AB}+h_{AB})

\displaystyle=0\,.

(H.12)

Given that the nonzero components of
h_{\mu\nu}
are

\displaystyle h_{rA}=\frac{2ma_{i}}{r^{n-2}f}{\mathring{\gamma}}_{AB}\delta^{B}_{\phi_{i}}\,,\quad h_{uA}=\frac{2ma_{i}}{r^{n-2}}{\mathring{\gamma}}_{AB}\delta^{B}_{\phi_{i}}\,.

(H.13)

The first two conditions are equivalent to

\displaystyle\partial_{r}\zeta^{u}-\frac{1}{2}h_{rr}

\displaystyle=0\,,

(H.14)

\displaystyle\partial_{r}\zeta^{A}-\frac{{\mathring{\gamma}}^{AB}}{r^{2}}\left(\partial_{B}\zeta^{u}-h_{rB}\right)

\displaystyle=0\,,

(H.15)

and one can check that
(
ChHMS
, Section 3)

\zeta^{r}=-\frac{1}{n-1}\left(r{\mathring{D}}_{B}\zeta^{B}+\frac{1}{2r}{\mathring{\gamma}}^{AB}h_{AB}\right)\,.

(H.16)

Given that
h_{rr}=0=h_{AB}
we can choose
\zeta^{u}=0
, and then the only non zero components of the vector are

\zeta^{A}=2ma_{i}\delta^{A}_{\phi_{i}}\int_{r_{0}}^{r}\frac{1}{s^{n}f}ds\,,

(H.17)

which puts the metric exactly into (
H.9
). Using that
\zeta^{A}
generates the infinitesimal transformation
x^{A}\to x^{A}+\xi^{A}
one can check that this is equivalent to (
H.8
).

Finally, identifying the angular momentum as
J_{i}=ma_{i}
,
clearly the linearised Kerr–(A)dS spacetime adds the following perturbations

\delta V=0=\delta\beta\,,\quad\delta U^{A}=-\frac{2J_{i}}{r^{n}}\,\delta^{A}_{\phi_{i}}\,.

(H.18)

In this form the (linearised) solution can easily be upgraded to
Ricci-flat
(\mathbf{S},\mathring{\gamma})

by replacing
f
with
\mathring{g}_{uu}:=-\alpha^{2}r^{2}-{2m}{r^{-(n-2)}}
, the metric on the sphere with the Ricci-flat metric on the transverse manifold
\mathbf{S}
, and setting

\delta U^{A}=\frac{\mathring{\lambda}^{A}(x^{C})}{r^{n}}\,,

(H.19)

where
\mathring{\lambda}_{A}(x^{C})
is an arbitrary Killing vector of the transverse space
\mathbf{S}
. One can then check that the linearised Einstein equations in Section
3
continue to hold for such a perturbation.

Appendix I
Some commutation relations

Let
h_{AB}
be a 2-covariant, symmetric trace-free tensor.
For the convenience of the reader we collect here several identities, some of which trivial, which are repeatedly used in the main body of the paper. Recall that
n=d+1
, where
d
is the dimension of the manifold carrying the Einstein metric
{\mathring{\gamma}}_{AB}
.

We have:

\displaystyle(i)

\displaystyle{\mathring{D}}_{B}\mathring{\Delta}\xi^{u}

\displaystyle=\mathring{\Delta}{\mathring{D}}_{B}\xi^{u}-(n-2){{\varepsilon}}{\mathring{D}}_{B}\xi^{u}

(I.1)

\displaystyle(ii)\,

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{TS}}[{\mathring{D}}_{A}\mathring{\Delta}\xi_{B}]

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathrm{TS}}[\mathring{\Delta}{\mathring{D}}_{A}\xi_{B}-(n-2){{\varepsilon}}{\mathring{D}}_{A}\xi_{B}+2\mathring{\mathscr{R}}({\mathring{D}}\xi)_{AB}]

(I.2)

\displaystyle(iii)\,

\displaystyle{\mathring{D}}^{B}\mathring{\Delta}\xi_{B}

\displaystyle=\mathring{\Delta}{\mathring{D}}^{A}\xi_{A}+(n-2){{\varepsilon}}{\mathring{D}}^{A}\xi_{A}

(I.3)

\displaystyle(iv)\,

\displaystyle{\mathring{D}}^{B}\mathring{\Delta}h_{AB}

\displaystyle=\mathring{\Delta}{\mathring{D}}^{B}h_{AB}-2{\mathring{D}}^{B}\mathring{\mathscr{R}}h_{AB}+(n-2){{\varepsilon}}{\mathring{D}}^{B}h_{AB}

(I.4)

\displaystyle(v)

\displaystyle{\mathring{D}}^{A}C(\xi)_{AB}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\bigg((\mathring{\Delta}+(n-2){{\varepsilon}})\xi_{B}+\frac{n-3}{n-1}{\mathring{D}}_{B}{\mathring{D}}_{C}\xi^{C}\bigg)

(I.5)

\displaystyle(vi)

\displaystyle{\mathring{D}}^{B}\mathop{\mathrm{TS}}[{\mathring{D}}_{A}{\mathring{D}}_{B}{\mathring{D}}^{C}\xi_{C}]

\displaystyle=\frac{n-2}{n-1}{\mathring{D}}_{A}\,[\,\mathring{\Delta}+(n-1){\varepsilon}]{\mathring{D}}_{C}\xi^{C}

(I.6)

\displaystyle(vii)

\displaystyle{\mathring{D}}_{A}D^{C}\mathring{\Delta}h_{BC}

\displaystyle=\mathring{\Delta}{\mathring{D}}_{A}D^{C}h_{BC}

\displaystyle+2\left(\mathring{R}^{E}{}_{A}{}^{F}{}_{B}P(h)_{EF}-D_{A}{\mathring{D}}^{C}\mathring{\mathscr{R}}(h)_{BC}+\frac{2(n-2)}{(n-1)}{{\varepsilon}}{\mathring{D}}^{E}D^{F}h_{EF}{\mathring{\gamma}}_{AB}\right)

(I.7)

Note, this last equation implies the commutation of the operators
\mathcal{K}\left({k},\mathring{\Delta},P\right)
.
Indeed, (
A.8
) shows
that we can write
\mathcal{K}\left({k},\mathring{\Delta},P\right)=A_{k}(B_{k}P+\mathring{\mathscr{R}}+\frac{1}{2}\mathring{\Delta}+C_{k})
for some numbers
A_{k}
,
B_{k}
and
C_{k}
.
Expanding the commutator gives

\displaystyle\left[\mathcal{K}\left({k},\mathring{\Delta},P\right),\mathcal{K}\left({k^{\prime}},\mathring{\Delta},P\right)\right](h)=A_{k}A_{k^{\prime}}(B_{k}-B_{k^{\prime}})[P,\mathring{\mathscr{R}}+\frac{1}{2}\mathring{\Delta}](h)=0\,,

(I.8)

where the second equality follows from applying the
\mathop{\mathrm{TS}}
operator to (
I
).

Appendix J
Further commutation relations

In this section we prove some further useful commutation relations.
We assume throughout that the metric
{\mathring{\gamma}}
is Einstein.

In the equations we often (implicitly) have the appearance of the following operator mapping symmetric trace-free tensors to themselves

\mathring{\Delta}_{T}h:=\left[\mathring{\Delta}+2(\mathring{\mathscr{R}}-(n-2){{\varepsilon}})\right]h\,.

(J.1)

This is the negative of the Lichnerowicz Laplacian.
Likewise, we have the “natural” Laplacians acting on vectors
V
and functions
\varphi

\displaystyle\mathring{\Delta}_{V}V

\displaystyle:=\left(\mathring{\Delta}-(n-2){{\varepsilon}}\right)V\,,

(J.2)

\displaystyle\mathring{\Delta}_{S}\varphi

\displaystyle:=\mathring{\Delta}\varphi\,,

(J.3)

which are “natural” because these are simply the (negative of) the Hodge Laplacian acting on one-forms and functions.

Now these are particularly useful in our context because they satisfy the following commutation properties:

\displaystyle\mathring{\Delta}_{T}\mathop{\mathrm{TS}}[{\mathring{D}}_{A}V_{B}]

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathrm{TS}}[{\mathring{D}}_{A}\mathring{\Delta}_{V}V_{B}]\,,

(J.4)

\displaystyle\mathring{\Delta}_{V}({\mathring{D}}_{A}\varphi)

\displaystyle={\mathring{D}}_{A}(\mathring{\Delta}_{S}\varphi)\,.

(J.5)

The first of these follows from (
I.2
) and (
3.92
), and the second from (
I.1
). Together, in particular, they imply

\displaystyle\mathring{\Delta}_{T}\mathop{\mathrm{TS}}[{\mathring{D}}_{A}{\mathring{D}}_{B}\varphi]

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathrm{TS}}[{\mathring{D}}_{A}{\mathring{D}}_{B}\mathring{\Delta}_{S}\varphi]\,.

(J.6)

It is a further useful result that

\displaystyle\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(2)}}\circ\mathring{\Delta}_{T}

\displaystyle=\mathring{\Delta}_{V}\circ\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(2)}}\,,

(J.7)

\displaystyle\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(1)}}\circ\mathring{\Delta}_{V}

\displaystyle=\mathring{\Delta}_{S}\circ\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(1)}}\,,

(J.8)

which follow from (
I.4
) and (
I.3
). Similarly this implies

\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(1)}}\circ\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(2)}}\circ\mathring{\Delta}_{T}=\mathring{\Delta}_{S}\circ\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(1)}}\circ\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(2)}}\,.

(J.9)

Now we can do a similar thing with the operator
P
. Defining

P_{T}\equiv P=C\circ\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(2)}}

(J.10)

to act specifically on symmetric trace-free two tensors, we can try to define operators
P_{V}
and
P_{S}
such that,

\displaystyle\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(2)}}\circ P_{T}

\displaystyle\equiv P_{V}\circ\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(2)}}\,,

(J.11)

\displaystyle\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(1)}}\circ P_{V}

\displaystyle\equiv P_{S}\circ\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(1)}}\,.

(J.12)

We have already seen (and it is obvious) that

P_{V}:=\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(2)}}\circ\,C

(J.13)

works.
Moreover, (
I.5
) and (
I.3
) together with (
J.12
) (see also (
C.55
)) imply

P_{S}\equiv P_{S}(\mathring{\Delta}_{S}):=\frac{n-2}{n-1}\left(\mathring{\Delta}_{S}+(n-1){{\varepsilon}}\right)\,.

(J.14)

Finally we can factor-in the decomposition
\xi=\xi^{[S]}+\xi^{[V]}
(cf. (
C.14
)), and act with
P_{V}
on these parts separately. This gives,

\displaystyle P_{V}(\xi^{[S]}+\xi^{[V]})

\displaystyle=P_{V}(\xi^{[S]})+P_{V}(\xi^{[V]})

(J.15)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\frac{n-2}{n-1}\left(\mathring{\Delta}_{V}+{{\varepsilon}}\right)}_{=:P_{V}^{[S]}(\mathring{\Delta}_{V})}\xi^{[S]}+\underbrace{\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathring{\Delta}_{V}+(n-2){{\varepsilon}}\right)}_{=:P_{V}^{[V]}(\mathring{\Delta}_{V})}\xi^{[V]}\,.

(J.16)

Here we have used (
I.5
) and (
C.14
) to obtain the second line.

Notice that
P_{V}
restricted to acting on
\xi^{[S]}

is just
P_{S}
promoted to act on vectors by replacing
\mathring{\Delta}_{S}
with
\mathring{\Delta}_{V}
. We can do the same trick to calculate the action of
P_{T}
on the scalar, vector, and tensor (transverse) parts of
h=h^{[S]}+h^{[V]}+h^{[{\mathrm{TT}}]}
(cf. (
C.12
)):

\displaystyle P_{T}(h^{[S]}+h^{[V]}+h^{[{\mathrm{TT}}]})

\displaystyle=P_{T}(h^{[S]})+P_{T}(h^{[V]})+P_{T}(h^{[{\mathrm{TT}}]})

(J.17)

\displaystyle=:P^{[S]}_{T}(h^{[S]})+P^{[V]}_{T}(h^{[V]})+P^{[{\mathrm{TT}}]}_{T}(h^{[{\mathrm{TT}}]})\,.

(J.18)

Using (
C.41
) and (
C.42
) we find

\displaystyle P^{[S]}_{T}

\displaystyle=P_{S}(\mathring{\Delta}_{T})\,,\quad P^{[V]}_{T}=P^{[V]}_{V}(\mathring{\Delta}_{T})\,,\quad P^{[{\mathrm{TT}}]}_{T}=0\,.

(J.19)

Now, since each of these operators depends only on the “natural” Laplacians we find the following commutation relations:

\displaystyle P_{T}\left(\mathop{\mathrm{TS}}[{\mathring{D}}_{A}V_{B}]\right)

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathrm{TS}}[{\mathring{D}}_{A}P_{V}V_{B}]\,,

(J.20)

\displaystyle P_{V}({\mathring{D}}_{A}\varphi)

\displaystyle={\mathring{D}}_{A}(P_{S}\varphi)\,,

(J.21)

\displaystyle P_{T}\left(\mathop{\mathrm{TS}}[{\mathring{D}}_{A}{\mathring{D}}_{B}\varphi]\right)

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathrm{TS}}[{\mathring{D}}_{A}{\mathring{D}}_{B}P_{S}\varphi]\,.

(J.22)

Consider, next, the operator
\mathcal{K}\left(k,\mathring{\Delta},P\right)
of (
A.6
). Assuming as usual that the metric
{\mathring{\gamma}}
is Einstein, rewritten in terms of the Lichnerowicz Laplacian
\mathring{\Delta}_{T}
we find

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}\left(k,\mathring{\Delta},P\right)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{7-n+2k}\bigg[\frac{2(n-1)P_{T}}{(3+k)(3-n+k)}+\mathring{\Delta}_{T}+(4+k(6-n+k)){{\varepsilon}}\bigg]

\displaystyle=:{\cal K}_{T}(k)\,.

(J.23)

We have:

\displaystyle\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(2)}}\circ{\cal K}_{T}(k)

\displaystyle={\cal K}_{V}(k)\circ\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(2)}}\,,

(J.24)

\displaystyle\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(1)}}\circ{\cal K}_{V}(k)

\displaystyle={\cal K}_{S}(k)\circ\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(1)}}\,,

(J.25)

\displaystyle\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(1)}}\circ\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(2)}}\circ{\cal K}_{T}(k)

\displaystyle={\cal K}_{S}(k)\circ\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(1)}}\circ\operatorname{{}\mathring{\text{d}iv}_{(2)}}\,.

(J.26)

where the subscript
{\cal K}_{X}(k)
indicates that operator is a function of the corresponding
\mathring{\Delta}_{X}
and
P_{X}
and maps
X\to X
.
Moreover these operators will respect the scalar, vector, and tensor decomposition in that

\displaystyle{\cal K}_{T}(k)\mathop{\mathrm{TS}}[{\mathring{D}}_{A}\xi_{B}]

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathrm{TS}}[{\mathring{D}}_{A}{\cal K}_{V}(k)\xi_{B}]\,,

(J.27)

\displaystyle{\cal K}_{V}(k)({\mathring{D}}_{A}\varphi)

\displaystyle={\mathring{D}}_{A}({\cal K}_{S}(k)\varphi)\,,

(J.28)

\displaystyle{\cal K}_{T}(k)\mathop{\mathrm{TS}}[{\mathring{D}}_{A}{\mathring{D}}_{B}\varphi]

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathrm{TS}}[{\mathring{D}}_{A}{\mathring{D}}_{B}{\cal K}_{S}(k)\varphi]\,.

(J.29)

Thus we can write the explicit expressions for
{\cal K}_{T}(k)
acting on

h^{[X]}
:

\displaystyle{\cal K}^{[S]}_{T}(k)

\displaystyle:=-\frac{(1+k)(5-n+k)}{(7-n+2k)(3-n+k)(3+k)}\left(\mathring{\Delta}_{T}+(2+k)(4-n+k){{\varepsilon}}\right)\,,

(J.30)

\displaystyle{\cal K}^{[V]}_{T}(k)

\displaystyle:=-\frac{(2+k)(4-n+k)}{(7-n+2k)(3-n+k)(3+k)}\left(\mathring{\Delta}_{T}+(1+k)(5-n+k){{\varepsilon}}\right)\,,

(J.31)

\displaystyle{\cal K}^{[{\mathrm{TT}}]}_{T}(k)

\displaystyle:=-\frac{1}{(7-n+2k)}\left(\mathring{\Delta}_{T}+[4+k(6-n+k)]{{\varepsilon}}\right)\,.

(J.32)

The expressions for
{\cal K}^{[X]}_{V,S}
acting on scalars
\varphi
and vectors
\xi
follow readily by replacing the Lichnerowicz Laplacian with the scalar or vector Laplacian
\mathring{\Delta}_{S}
or
\mathring{\Delta}_{V}
from (
J.2
) and (
J.3
) respectively.
In this form it is clear that whenever the prefactor is defined,
the operators are elliptic since their principal part is the Laplacian.

Note that
k=-3
is not allowed in
{\cal K}_{V}(k)
and
{\cal K}_{S}(k)
, but
k=-3
does not appear when trying to set-up a recurrence for the vector and scalar parts of the transport equations (
4.17
).
Next, one checks that
{\cal K}^{[{\mathrm{TT}}]}_{T}(-3)
, coincides with the expression for the special case
k=-3
of (
A.11
) restricted to act on
h^{[{\mathrm{TT}}]}
. It follows that one can use these transport equations for each field
\overset{(i)}{q}{}_{AB}^{[X]}
independently, and that the operators appearing in the recursions are only those in (
J.30
)-(
J.32
).

We finally note that (
J.30
)-(
J.32
) can be grouped together. If
s=0,1,2
for scalars, vectors, and
{\mathrm{TT}}
tensors respectively, then

{\cal K}^{[X_{s}]}_{T}(k)\equiv-\frac{(1+s+k)(5-s-n+k)}{(7-n+2k)(3-n+k)(3+k)}\\
\times\left(\mathring{\Delta}_{T}+[s^{2}(s-1)+(2-s+k)(4+s-n+k)]{{\varepsilon}}\right)\,.

(J.33)

Clearly, when
s=0,1
(scalars and vectors) these operators vanish on the subspaces when
k=-(s+1)
. This implies that the scalar and the vector parts drop out of the recursion relations for the
\overset{(i)}{q}_{AB}
’s.
